#7013. I Tried a Korean Lash Lift. Did It Really Live Up to the Hype?

The buzzy treatment promises lifted, fluttery lashes without extensions. Here are my results.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Confession: I’ll try almost anything in the name of longer, more fluttery lashes. That’s especially true if the thing shortens my makeup routine and gets me out the door a little faster in the morning (read: lets me stay in bed even just a few minutes longer). So I was, of course, very excited when I discovered a beauty treatment that promised to accomplish both: the Korean lash lift.

I’d previously heard some not-so-great things about lash lifts. The chemical solution used during the process—typically ammonium thioglycolate (TGA)—comes with a risk of dryness, breakage, and damage, especially if the lift is performed incorrectly or too frequently. But Korean lash lifts have been gaining serious attention (Pinterest reports that searches for the aesthetic treatment are up more than 400 percent) as a potentially gentler alternative, since they can feature formulas and techniques designed to minimize stress on the lashes.

And as someone with stubbornly straight lashes, I was particularly intrigued. To me, nothing ruins a good makeup day quite like lashes that refuse to cooperate, and trust me, I’ve put in the work to find products that make mine behave. If you’re wondering, after testing dozens of eyelash curlers and mascaras, I’ve landed on my undefeated duo: the Kevyn Aucoin Eyelash Curler and Tarte Tartelette Tubing Mascara. But even those two can’t always convince my lashes to hold a curl all day.

So, when I learned that a Korean lash lift could potentially give me weeks of lifted, fluttery lashes, no curler required, I knew I had to try it. Now that I’ve lived with the results for two months—including through a vacation, a wedding, and an apartment move—I have a lot of thoughts.

So, first things first: A Korean lash lift isn’t the same thing as eyelash extensions. Rather than attaching individual fibers to your natural lashes, the treatment uses chemical solutions to reshape the lashes you already have to give them a lifted, curled appearance. It’s the same basic concept as getting a perm for a whole head of curls, but on a much smaller scale.

That technique is similar to traditional lash lifts; the biggest distinction typically comes down to the process and products used. For example, traditional lash lifts tend to use perming formulas with ammonium thioglycolate (TGA), whereas Korean ones usually use cysteamine hydrochloride. The latter chemical is often touted as a gentler or safer alternative, but the truth of the matter is that “we do not yet have sufficient high-quality comparative clinical studies demonstrating that they consistently produce fewer ocular complications than traditional thioglycolate-based lash lifts,” says ophthalmologist Sheri Rowen, MD.

Korean lash lifts are also glueless, meaning they use balm instead of glue to get them to stick to the shield that makes the lashes stand up and have that classic curl.

“Korean lash lifts are often designed with an emphasis on more controlled processing and conditioning of the lashes, which may reduce some of the dryness and brittleness associated with chemical treatments,” says Dr. Rowen. However, this doesn’t automatically mean it’s better or safer than any traditional lash lift; all that depends on how the perming chemicals are applied.

“My overall recommendation is that patients should not assume a lash lift is safer simply because it is labeled ‘Korean,’ ‘gentle,’ or ‘conditioning,’” says Dr. Rowen. “The most important considerations are the ingredients, proper application, careful protection of the ocular surface, and avoiding excessive or repeated chemical processing.”

Still, if the treatment is done well, you can expect a natural-looking lift that makes your existing lashes appear longer, more separated, and more defined without the need for extensions.

For my appointment, I visited Two of a Kind, a lash and brow studio in Santa Monica that offers lash lifts, specifically Korean and what their team calls “hybrid,” meaning they use techniques from both methods to customize the results. The entire treatment took about 40 minutes, although my lash technician mentioned that she’s particularly quick. If you’re planning an appointment, I’d budget closer to an hour or potentially longer, depending on your technician and the specific service.

It was my first time ever getting a lash lift, so I didn’t have anything to compare it to. But Two of a Kind’s founder Jordan Feise told me another difference between the traditional and Korean methods: The former uses one shield that shapes the lashes throughout the appointment. The latter uses two shields, always starting with a flat shield (to straighten the lashes and get closer to the root for more lift, Feise explains), then swapping in a curved shield (to lock in the shape and create a curl, she adds). By using two shields, it separates the softening step and the shaping step, which Feise says gives the technician more control over how your lashes ultimately look. “Using two shields during a lash lift allows us to customize the shape of the lashes during the treatment for a more lifted, natural-looking result,” says Feise. “Together, they give us more control over the final result, helping create a beautiful lift without over-curling the lashes.”

Here’s how the appointment went for me: I laid down and my lash tech cleansed my lashes, removing any makeup, product residue, and oil to prep them for treatment. Then, the flat shield was applied to my lid along the upper lash line. She also made sure to separate my lower lashes from my upper ones. Next, the lifting begins. The initial lift is done using balm and the flat shield, which is a thin, flat pad (instead of a curved rod you’d see with a traditional lash lift). This straightens and lifts the lashes. After processing, the tech switched to that traditional curved lift shield to shape and curl. More processing, then neutralizing the formula (to make sure my eyes/contacts wouldn’t sting after), and plenty of conditioning, and I was all set.

During the appointment, I did what any beauty editor would do: I asked approximately a million questions.

How long would my results last? Around two months, she told me. Could I wear mascara and eye shadow afterward? Absolutely, after the initial 24-hour aftercare period. What about getting my lashes wet? Also fine after 24 hours. And would I have to do anything special to maintain the results?

Not really. She recommended applying a lash conditioner a few times during the first couple of weeks to keep the hairs soft and supple. (Full disclosure: I was a bad patient and only remembered to use it once. I still had great results.)

Basically, after the first 24 hours, I could go back to my normal routine—showering, washing my face, wearing eye makeup, and all—without worrying about ruining my lashes. “Even if I sleep on my face?” I asked. Yep, even then.

I have to admit, I was skeptical. The idea that my lashes could stay perfectly curled through weeks of sleeping, showering, and generally living my life seemed a little too good to be true, honestly. But I was very excited to find out.

Spoiler alert: I was so, so wrong to doubt.

For two months straight, my lashes looked unbelievably long, lifted, and curled, yet still felt soft and looked completely natural. And the best part? I didn’t have to do much of anything to keep them that way.

In fact, I ended up putting my new lashes through what I can only describe as an endurance test. First, there was my birthday trip to Hawaii, where I spent every day swimming in the ocean. Then came a wedding, where plenty of happy tears were shed. Throw in more travel, moving apartments, and a whole lot of stress-induced eye rubbing, and let’s just say I wasn’t exactly treating my lashes like delicate little flowers. Yet, through all of it, they kept their curl.

Initially, I skipped mascara altogether because my lashes looked so good on their own. But because I hadn’t paired my lift with a lash tint—a popular add-on that darkens your lashes—I eventually started incorporating mascara back into my routine for a little extra definition.

The one thing that stayed the same throughout the months was that I no longer needed my eyelash curler. Like, at all. Instead of spending time trying to convince my lashes to cooperate, I could simply run a clean spoolie through them to separate each lash, swipe on a little mascara, and head out the door. A dream come true.

And when I didn’t feel like wearing makeup, a little tinted sunscreen or moisturizer was all I needed to feel put together because my lashes already looked like I’d spent time on them. You know that TikTok trend that’s all about the high-maintenance things we do to stay low-maintenance? Yeah, a Korean lash lift falls squarely in that category.

That was probably my favorite part of it all: The results were noticeable without looking done. Of course, I didn’t have the dramatic, obviously enhanced look of extensions, but my natural lashes had enough lift and definition to make my eyes look more awake and fluttery. It was effortless, understated glam, which is exactly my go-to for my everyday makeup.

By the three-month mark, my lashes had gradually returned to their natural (read: stubby and stick-straight) state. But I was very happy to notice zero brittleness, unusual fallout, or other obvious signs of damage. A win!

That would be a resounding yes. After experiencing nearly three happy months with my Korean lash lift, I can confidently say that it’s one of the most worthwhile beauty treatments I’ve tried in a long time. I absolutely loved the look, the experience, all of it.

All this is coming from someone whose job is to constantly test new products and treatments in the name of beauty journalism. Plenty of them deliver impressive results, but far fewer actually make my everyday life easier. And even fewer genuinely excite me. This one did all three.

I loved how natural my lashes looked, how well the curl held up through trials and tribulations, and how little maintenance was required. But what really sold me was the convenience. Being able to wake up with lashes that already looked lifted and fluttery—and shave several minutes off my makeup routine in the process—was something I appreciated every single morning. It just made me feel so much more confident. One thing to note: It can get pricey. At Two of a Kind Brows, one appointment will set you back $120 for a lift and $160 for a lift and tint.

Would I recommend a Korean lash lift? I’d say it’s absolutely worth it, provided you go to a qualified professional and don’t overdo it by getting lash lifts too often. But once you’ve tried one, you won’t look back.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered all things skincare, makeup, haircare, and nails and has tested so many products and treatments that she’s lost count.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#7009. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

Why we love it: Glow Recipe is one of the internet’s favorite skincare brands, but the pretty packaging isn’t all that the shelf-worthy products have to offer. Korean skincare is so good because K-beauty brands embody a skin-first philosophy. “At a young age, Korean children are taught the importance of moisturizing, exfoliating, cleansing the skin thoroughly, and protecting their skin with SPF,” says , K-beauty expert Charlotte Cho. The more time you invest in taking care of your skin and understanding what products and ingredients work for you, the better results you will see, she explains.

K-beauty is also about accessibility. “The most innovative Korean skincare products do not break the bank—you can get effective ingredients and cutting-edge formulas that everyone can afford,” Cho says. This philosophy ensures that everyone— regardless of budget—gets to prioritize the health of their skin.

“Innovation and effectiveness have become synonymous with K-beauty; Korean skincare products are always searching for the best, most beneficial way to help treat and prevent concerns,” Cho explains. Because this is the case, K-beauty brands are often pioneering a ton of new and exciting ingredients, textures, delivery systems, and categories (snail mucin! sorbet soft cleansing balms! cushion compacts! essences!).

“Also, many of the ingredients that we’ve come to love and associate with Korean skincare come from hanbang, or in other words, traditional Korean medicine—ginseng, green tea, and lotus, all have long histories of being used in Korean remedies,” Cho says.

Each and every person’s skin is unique, meaning some may benefit from a 10-step routine, and others may not. Cho agrees, “Learning about the 10 steps is a great educational resource and tool for you to understand what possibilities there are, but I firmly believe that the number of steps in your routine is up to the individual lifestyle, budget, and skin goals.”

After reading and learning about all of these incredible K-beauty products, I can understand why you may want to jump in and pop each and every one in your cart. But slow down! (1) Adding too much too soon can freak your skin out, and (2) let’s be real, that’s a sh-t-ton of money. Start slow and begin with a ritual that can be easily incorporated into your skincare routine. Cho’s advice? Double-cleansing.

“One of my favorite parts of my skincare routine is double-cleansing; it’s the foundation of any skincare routine—the first step removes oil-based gunk, like sunscreen and makeup, and the second step ensures the remaining water-based gunk, like sweat and dirt, are gone,” says Cho. She explains that this method is the best way to remove bacteria and oil that can lead to breakouts and premature aging.

Charlotte Cho is the founder of the skincare line Then I Met You and cofounder of the incredibly popular K-beauty site Soko Glam.

#7003. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

#6999. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

#6997. Reviewing Ross Lynch’s Low-Key Relationship History Now That He’s a Married Man (!!)

Yes, his wife’s identity is still mostly a mystery.

If you missed the headlines this morning, Ross Lynch is now a married man…and if you’re thinking, “Um, I didn’t even know he was dating anyone,” you’re not alone. Despite hard-launching his relationship status at the Heartland premiere—telling People, “Yes, yes I am,” when asked if he was married—the actor and musician has yet to even confirm his wife’s name, much less how they met or how long they’ve been together.

Luckily, I’ve done the work of piecing together Ross’s very low-key relationship history for you, from his Teen Beach Movie love triangle, to his six-year relationship with his Chilling Adventures of Sabrina costar, to his mysterious new wife (!!). Scroll ahead for everything I’ve uncovered.

Okay, it’s unclear if these two actually dated, but Ross did tell Cosmo (hi!) during an April 2019 interview that he was involved in “a love triangle” on the set of Disney’s Teen Beach Movie.

“I was 16 at the time of the first movie, but it was basically a bunch of high school students or college students,” he told Cosmo (via Seventeen). “And they just put us on an island in the Caribbean for two months.”

He never named who was involved in the love triangle, but this flirty Instagram pic ^^ that he posted with backup dancer Morgan Larson in July 2013 led fans to believe that something possibly went down between the two.

Again, this one was only ever rumored, but fans suspect that Ross’s Teen Beach Movie costar Maia was possibly the other person involved in the supposed “love triangle.” The reason? While chatting with Hollywood Life in June 2015, Ross shared that he and Maia had an unscripted kiss in the sequel film.

“Maia and I did a kiss spontaneously,” he said. “We thought that Mac and Brady should have an onscreen kiss. It wasn’t scripted and the director didn’t tell us to do it and we just did it and hopefully it makes it in the movie!” Oh, and he also added that he thought Maia was a good kisser. Moving on!

In September 2015, Ross became Instagram-official with Yellowjackets star Courtney after reportedly meeting on the set of the movie Status Update. The two went on to date for just about two years before going their separate ways, with Courtney being described as Ross’s “then-girlfriend” during a November 2017 interview with W.

A month later in December 2017, Ross and Courtney were spotted having lunch together, but it doesn’t seem like they ever officially reconciled.

Dating rumors about these two began shortly after Ross and Jaz met on the set of Chilling Adventures of Sabrina, but they really ramped up after Ross’s sister Rydel shared a Snapchat of Ross and Jaz kissing on New Year's Eve in 2018. However, when asked about their relationship by Entertainment Tonight later that month, Ross insisted that Jaz was just his “closest friend.” Mmkay.

Just a few months later in April 2019, Us Weekly caught the couple getting cozy in NYC, and at long last in January 2020, the costars finally made their red carpet debut together at the Balmain show in Paris.

The couple kept fans swooning for the next couple of years until June 2023, when a fan shared a video of Lynch discussing heartbreak during one of his live shows with his band The Driver Era, sparking breakup rumors. “The thing about heartbreak is the only thing that really helps is music,” he told the audience at the time.

Following his split from Jaz (and rumors of a brief relationship with a woman named Indie Wolper), Ross seemingly soft-launched a new romance on TikTok in September 2024, which sent fans into a frenzy.

A few months later, Ross hinted that there was someone new in his life during a January 2025 interview with radio show Smallzy’s Surgery. Talking about The Driver Era song “Same Old Story,” Ross said, “It’s like really recent—like, maybe, I’m talking like a month ago. It’s about this experience I’ve been having recently with this girl.”

Reddit sleuths got to work and ID’d the new mystery woman as Anna, claiming the two met in Portugal. By early 2026, fans took to Reddit to claim that—when asked about his ring at a meet-and-greet—Ross confirmed he’d been married since before Christmas 2025.

Then, after a nearly year-long hiatus from Instagram, Ross reappeared on social media in June 2026, with his first photo showing him wearing what appeared to be said wedding ring.

“Been a minute, what did I miss?” he captioned the post, and when a fan slid into his comments to write, “more like what did WE miss ? 🤨💍” Ross replied, “hahaha A LOT 🤫.”

Finally, Ross cleared things up at the premiere of his new movie Heartland in November 2026, telling People, “Yes, yes I am,” when asked if he was married.

“I’m not trying to shy away from that by any means, but I am trying to, you know, my wife’s not very fond of celebrity,” he said of keeping his relationship on the down-low. “So I am trying to be sensitive towards that and respectful to her. But everything’s going well.”

#6996. Designers Say These Are the 10 Best Antiques to Find at Estate Sales

Thrift stores and flea markets are popular spots for finding unusual vintage pieces, but they’re not the only options. Estate sales let you browse antiques in the very homes where they’ve been used and collected over the years. And since many items haven’t passed through a dealer, they may cost less than similar finds at an antique shop.

That combination makes estate sales a favorite among interior designers. They’re a source of characterful pieces for both client projects and personal collections. Krystal Reinhard, founder and principal designer of Old Soul Design Studio, likes being able to see items in the settings where they’ve long belonged. “It makes it easier to spot quality, scale, and patina,” she says.

For Valerie Helgeson, founder and lead designer at Design Directions, the appeal of estate sales is the fast-paced nature of the hunt. “There’s an immediacy to them that I like. You have to make a decision and trust your instincts,” she shares.

Whether you’re after a bargain, an authentic antique, or the fun of finding an unexpected treasure, estate sales are worth exploring. Ahead, interior designers reveal the 10 antiques they always look for—and explain why each belongs on your estate sale wish list.

Paige Loperfido, founder and principal designer of Decor & More Design Studio, is always game to grab hand-painted porcelain, midcentury ceramics, and antique glassware, like ice buckets and goblets.

“These collectibles bring color, pattern, and personality to kitchens and dining rooms,” she says. “Beyond their practical use, they easily transition into art, with vintage plates mounted on walls for a dynamic display, or grouped on open shelves. In dining spaces, glassware and tableware act as statement pieces, highlighting one-of-a-kind treasures that feel timeless, sophisticated, and thoughtfully curated, adding both charm and character to special gatherings.”

Reinhard calls antique candlestick holders “an instant layer,” with their warmth, height, and “a little romance with a surprisingly big impact for a small spend.” She adds that she often styles them as matched pairs on mantels and consoles, or as loose trios at varying heights on dining tables and coffee tables.

Going into estate sales with an open mind, Helgeson typically finds herself buying something she “could have never imagined looking for.” But frequently, one item tops her wish list, and that’s copper kitchenware. She displays these antique pieces in kitchens alongside other estate sale–found items, such as turned-wood bowls, old cutting boards, and bread bowls.

Pottery is a designer darling, since these pieces add texture and even imperfection (in a good way) to spaces, as Reinhard puts it. She tracks down pottery at estate sales, then showcases it on open shelving, sideboards, and tablescapes. She prefers to pair pottery with “something refined,” like brass, stone, or glass, to keep the mix elevated.

Loperfido’s favorite estate sale gems are what she calls “culturally rich pieces” that have been collected by homeowners from around the world. Think: Chinese antiques like foo dogs and ginger jars, and vintage ceramics such as majolica fish candlesticks, figurative pieces, or midcentury glazed pottery.

Oftentimes, she says that these particular pieces “showcase incredible craftsmanship and character,” adding that they’re perfect to layer on surfaces throughout the house, from bookshelves to nightstands to end tables.

At estate sales, Helgeson is scanning the spaces for “furniture with a strong silhouette.” Acting as anchors in rooms, Helgeson believes in accessorizing the rest of these spaces with vintage finds, which she says makes even new pieces appear like collected treasures.

“Vintage rugs bring color, pattern, and age instantly,” Reinhard remarks. She decorates with them “everywhere”: in powder rooms, mudrooms, and under seating areas to anchor the furniture and add depth.

“Original art can feel inaccessible at gallery pricing, but estate sales are one of the best places to find something truly special for a fraction of the cost,” Reinhard points out. She goes for single statement pieces, small collected clusters, or creates a full gallery wall that combines vintage art with modern pieces and personal photos.

Continually on the lookout for leather-bound collections and cocktail books, Reinhard snaps up antique books at estate sales.

“They’re one of the easiest ways to add personality and story—especially art, history, and travel titles that feel timeless and intentional,” she says. “I use them everywhere—stacked on coffee tables, tucked onto consoles, layered into shelves, or used as risers for objects to create height and visual rhythm.”

Loperfido says that these “finely detailed etched artworks” are often overlooked at estate sales, but they can instantly add sophistication and historical texture to any space.

“Small but rich in visual storytelling, they work beautifully against neutral or patterned backgrounds,” she notes.

#6995. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

#6990. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

#6988. 7 Desert Boots That Can Survive the Winter

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Despite the name, these chukkas are built to be worn in all conditions.

Winter weather can get ugly. If it’s not wind and rain, it’s slushy sidewalks and unexpected snow flurries. You obviously need shoes that can handle a little precipitation, but the key phrase here is a little. You don’t want to stomp around in heavy winter boots, especially if we want to look nice. Dress shoes are a tough fit in winter, as are dress boots. Funny enough, between December and March, you know what our go-to footwear becomes? Desert boots. Technically chukka boots, but a few desert boots make the cut here.

But that’s something to note. The Esquire office does not use the term interchangeably—but the Internet does, so we’re going along with it. We know some of you—nerds, mostly—have stopped reading this article because you don’t think we know the difference between the two. We’re well aware of the square and rectangle-ism: All desert boots are chukkas, not all chukkas are desert boots.

If you’re not a nerd and want to learn something new, chukkas are ankle-high boots with open lacing and two or three pairs of eyelets, often made with leather uppers—leather or rubber soled. Desert boots are a subsection of this silhouette that typically features a suede upper (nubuck, sometimes) with lighter-colored, more flexible crepe soles. They are not as winterized—suede, especially, is known to take a beating from moisture—hence the name. The details vary here and there, but both styles can work for winter if you choose wisely.

We found seven pairs of chukka boots—yes, some of which are desert boots—with weather-friendly features, from water-resistant uppers to sturdy rubber soles. Most have both, so you can find a pair that looks sharp without leaving you entirely at the mercy of the winter weather forecast.

These chukka boots might as well be dress boots. They look sharp no matter what outfit you pair them with, and the ultra-refined, premium calfskin leather upper is naturally water-resistant; to a point, that is—we wouldn’t wear them in the pouring rain.

They’re very comfortable thanks to a soft, leather lining on the inside and open-cell foam insoles that provide supportive cushioning. And the flexible outsoles made from XL Extralight injection molding are a nice touch that give the boot some lightweight flexibility.

For a classic pair of chukka boots that come at a good price and have a wide range of colors and sizes, Thursday Boot Co. is the way to go. These chukkas come in a variety of colors and materials, but our favorite has to be the Mocha colorway in weather-safe suede. It’s rare to find a pair of suede shoes you can actually get wet without ruining, but these ones can brave the weather while still looking great. And with studded rubber outsoles, you have some extra traction and durability. If you want even more traction and durability in the soles, try one of the Rugged & Resilient styles with anti-slip wedge rubber outsoles.

Here’s another suede option you can actually wear during the winter; Sabah treats the suede upper of this boot to be water resistant, but you can still add a suede treatment for extra protection if you want to. These boots are handmade in Monte San Giusto, Italy, and you can trust that they were made with lots of skill and care. The insole and midsole are made from vegetable tanned leather, so they end up forming to your feet once you wear them, making them extra comfortable.

If you like the look of a gum-sole sneaker, you might like how it looks on a boot, too. With these desert boots, SeaVees blends the classic gum-sole look with the more rugged desert boot style, and the result is a versatile boot that can withstand pretty much any weather. With water-resistant rugged oiled leather uppers and natural rubber soles, these guys can get you through quite a lot.

These are going to be the least weather-ready on this list. You don’t want to take them out in a snowstorm, but damn do they look good. Imagine these worn to a holiday party, worn with a nice pair of chinos and fair-isle sweater.

The suede upper is a true not-water-resistant suede. But they do have durable rubber soles. The stitched moc-toe is a fun detail that makes these chukka boots stand out from the rest of the pack. Drake’s notes that these boots run small, so they recommend sizing up by a half-size when ordering online.

We’re starting to get pretty far away from the chukka heart, but you can see the chukka-shaped vamp and the ankle-y shape. Plus, when we think of rugged workwear boots, we think of Timberland. The upper is made from waterproof leather and has a TimberDry waterproof membrane for extra protection, and you can see that Gripstick rubber outsole gives you some distance from the ground and provides great traction as well.

And finally, we can’t talk about chukka boots without mentioning Clarks. The original Clarks desert boot was designed in 1949 and inspired by the rough suede and crepe-soled boots worn by off-duty British soldiers during WWII (as well as the shoes worn by polo players in India and South African veldskoen shoes). Today, the brand’s original style is still going strong, and you can get a pair for under $100.

#6986. The 7 Best Nail Trends to Try Right Now

From “Winter Wine” to “Tiger’s Eye,” these looks are perfect for colder months ahead

It’s a little too early for holiday nail art manicures, and maybe even too late for a fall palette of maples, golds, and pumpkin polish shades. So, what to do in the weeks of November ahead when it comes to your nail look?

“November calls for rich, warming shades such as chocolate brown, mocha, espresso, and caramel, alongside deep cherry, oxblood, and olive green,” says Townhouse founder Juanita Huber-Millet. “Our Flawless collection captures the season beautifully, with coffee-inspired shades like Chai Latte and Mocha, and timeless reds such as Red Apple and Cherry for an effortlessly chic fall manicure.”

Huber-Millet also recommends burgundy, classic red, and deep plum for a timeless winter shade. “This season, expect modern twists with glossy chrome and cat-eye finishes, colored French tips and delicate gems that add a festive touch without compromising on sophistication,” the professional adds.

Below, discover our edit of the best nail trends and colors to wear this November and beyond, including gemstone-inspired cat-eye shades and modern, high-shine neutrals to suit any occasion.

A rich, high-shine black-brown shade is the new classic color for the season. The key is to opt out of milkier shades and lean into depth, with crisp, clean cuticles and a generously glossy top coat.

Dress up purple and burgundy shades with subtle polka dots, embracing the year’s most playful nail art trend with a sophisticated, grown-up edge. The key is to keep the dots spacious and minimal.

A clean, black manicure is as much a timeless winter staple as a gray layering piece or a pair of leather boots. The key is to keep your nails short and smart with a soft-square shape, and pay extra attention to hydrating and tidying your cuticles.

Trade cow print and leopard spots for a more elevated, minimal zebra print this November. The best method is to put a playful twist on the classic French tip manicure, using fine lines to create subtle definition.

November’s take on the burgundy manicure is the darkest, glossiest yet. Pair deep, shiny pinotage tones with a classic shape, like almond or soft-square, and embrace purple-leaning hues over classic reds.

“Chrome and cat-eye finishes are key trends this winter,” says Huber-Millet. “At Townhouse, we’re seeing brown chrome become a firm favorite, combining rich, chocolatey tones with a luminous finish for a manicure that feels luxurious, contemporary, and perfectly suited to the season.”

The result? A multidimensional finish evocative of the classic tiger’s-eye gemstone.

Skip natural pink chrome and cat-eye this season and embrace rich, dark bases, from black to brown to burgundy. For a wintry twist, opt for a reflective blue or silver sheer chrome that allows the depth to shine through while highlighting its unique shift.

#6985. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

#6984. We’ve Tested Virtually Every At-Home Laser Hair Removal Device—These 9 Stood Out

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

See the best picks of 2026, how they work, the results, and the deals you shouldn't miss.

Here’s my unfiltered review of laser hair removal: It’s painful, expensive, and takes a lot of sessions to see results. Soon, I'm running right back to waxing (ouch) and shaving (annoying). I thought at-home laser hair removal devices would be an expensive little beauty gadget that did nothing—yet another tool piled up next to my abandoned skincare devices. But I was so wrong. They're actually the best of both worlds.

An at-home laser hair removal device actually can help reduce the number of hairs that grow on you by damaging the follicle with every zap—all from your bed, couch, bathroom, wherever. This makes your hair look sparser, creating the illusion that you have far less hair (and making hair removal a whole lot quicker). And yep, at-home laser hair removal devices do work. They just use lower energy levels than the professional devices at a medspa or dermatologist’s office, which means they also require way more treatments to get similar results.

I’ve tried out all the top devices on the market for two months, evaluating how easy they were to use, how long they take on different areas of the body, the level of pain, and the efficacy, all to come up with this list of the top 10 out there. Here’s a peek at our top favorites.

Keep reading for a bit more info on these devices, plus a full review of all of our picks (like how we use them, the results we've seen in two months, and more).

Safety Guidelines and Skin Type Considerations:

Annoyingly, there are currently no at-home laser hair removal devices that are safe to use on dark skin and/or light hair, even if the device claims to work for “everyone.” In reality, they won’t work—or are unsafe for—any skin tones deeper than “light brown” or on hair colors lighter than “medium brown.” So make sure to speak with your dermatologist before wasting your money or, worse, damaging your skin. Got it? Good.

Now keep reading for info on how at-home laser hair removal works, the difference between IPL devices and laser devices, and first-person reviews on the best ones to try.

Why we love it: After four months of consistent testing, this Philips IPL device quickly rose in the ranks as the best laser hair removal device we’ve tried. It’s seriously high-tech, which can sometimes make something more complicated, but in this instance, it ups the beginner-friendly factor by miles. Once you connect the device to the accompanying app, the device actually speaks to you as you’re using it. So as you glide it down your leg or under your arms, the device will say if you’re doing it correctly or if you missed a spot.

Each attachment is geared toward a different body area (body—like arms and legs—face, bikini, and underarms), and they also use smart sensors to adjust the treatment to your skin’s needs, taking into account your skin tone and hair color. But the key to IPL hair removal is consistency, though, and the app actually tracks your progress and will send you a notification when you’re due for another treatment.

Cosmo editor's review: "I actually flew to Amsterdam in June to get a first sneak peek before this was available in America last month. I’ve been testing it ever since, and it has truly changed my hair removal entirely. I’m no stranger to laser hair removal devices; I’ve actually been using them on and off for six years. But this worked the fastest of anything I’ve tried. In just two treatments, spaced two weeks apart, I noticed less hair under my arms. Now, I only use it once a month or so, and my hair barely grows. I realized I have a pile of shaving creams to test, and I haven’t used a single one yet.

But the results aren’t the only thing that’s kept me consistent. I also just love how easy this is to use. Especially on areas like my bikini line, where I can’t really see what I’m doing, the device telling me that I’m doing it right—and when I’m doing it wrong—has absolutely made a difference. I only just started using this on my bikini line, as I was waiting until summer was over to avoid any potential irritation, but I’m quickly seeing some promising results. ”—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: A handful of Cosmo editors have tested the Ulike laser hair removal devices over the years, and every single person has shared rave reviews. And after testing five different devices on myself (doing hard work over here), I can confidently say the Air 3 model is the best one. It's affordable, extremely easy to use and set up, doesn't hurt, and comes with everything you need (including a mini razor to shave with before starting). Lemme get into it:

First of all, Ulike uses a unique "ice cooling" technology that essentially cools down your skin as it zaps you, cutting down on any pain and making your treatment really comfortable. To make it easy to use, the device has three different modes: "Soft mode" for your face and upper lip, "body mode" for your arms and legs, and "power mode" for your under arms and crotch—all of which are designed to work the best on these areas based on the thickness of skin and hair (think about it: your upper lip doesn't need the same power as your pubes). And when you actually use the tool, you can stamp it against your skin and press the button, or glide it across and it'll auto-zap you (which definitely trims treatment length).

Cosmo editor's review: "I'm no stranger to laser hair removal devices and have used a handful over the years. I really didn't think I could love one this much, though (I should have listened to my coworkers, lol). I've been putting this to use on my underarms where my skin is sensitive and gets irritated so easily. And I've never noticed any issues. Best of all, though, it's so easy to use that I've actually remained consistent. I am quick to stop doing things once they become complicated (I'm a Sagittarius! I can't help it!), but all I have to do is shave, plug this in, turn it on, and start zappin'. There aren't any useless buttons to press or apps to connect to. And the key to getting the results you want—i.e. fewer hairs over time—is keeping up with your treatments. I've used this now for two months, and I definitely am picking up the razor less. Now, I pretty much only shave when I'm going to do a treatment."—BG

Why we love it: Like the Dyson of laser hair removal devices, the Braun Skin i-Expert won the Cosmo Holy Grail Beauty Award in2024. The device pairs with a free app to take you through the whole process, so it's fantastic if you're totally new to this whole thing and need some guided help. The app also keeps track of all of your treatments so you know how often you're using it to really nab your results. There are also two methods based on different areas of your skin: A stamping method that's great for getting more precise areas, and a gliding method that you can use on your legs or underarms.

It comes with a lot (which certainly helps make up for the price). There are four heads (two precision caps for face and your bikini line, one wide cap for your body, and one standard size, all of which have skin tone and hair color sensors to avoid any potential skin damage), plus a Gillette Venus razor—aka a very good one—to shave with beforehand and a chic carrying case to store everything in.

FWIW, this Braun device is highly loved on Reddit, especially by users with tan to medium skin tones who normally find they can’t use most IPL devices without damaging their skin. One reviewer said they love that the device automatically adjusts the settings based on their skin tone, which tends to be on the low side for their medium skin. They said they used it twice weekly for five months and noticed that they barely have any hair on their underarms, legs, and bikini line these days, and even the hair that does grow comes back very slowly. So while, yes, it’s an investment, it’s a whole lot more ~fancy~ than the others on this list—and still a fraction of what you’d spend on laser hair removal treatments in-office, FYI.

One Cosmo editor's review: "I was a laser hair removal virgin, except when I tried getting my under-arms professionally lasered in high school, but my pain tolerance was too low to do more than two sessions, so I was super excited to try an at-home laser device. I loathe shaving, so I'll usually use an epilator or wax my underarms. I will say upon first impression, I loved how Braun's device came with allll the bells and whistles—including a razor. The case itself is very compact, which I also appreciated as a beauty editor with tons of tools living in a small NYC apartment. After setting up the app for about 10 minutes, the rest of the process was seamless. The device is super techy and does the adjusting for you, so all I had to do was hold the device on my skin and wait for it to flash. It wasn't completely painless, but it hurt less than my professional appointment, and a lot less than an epilator. It's safe to say that I'm officially an at-home laser convert."

We’ve been testing at-home laser hair removal devices for five years, bringing you the best ones with all of our firsthand experience and updating whenever we try new launches or underrated gems. To compile this full list, we:

Laser hair removal is a process where specific wavelengths of light are directed onto your skin in little pulses, targeting and heating up the hair follicle to destroy a portion of it with each treatment, says dermatologist Arash Akhavan, MD. Compared to in-office treatments (which, although pricey, are done by board-certified derms or trained professionals who know exactly what they’re doing), at-home devices have a much lower energy output—mostly because they often use IPL (intense pulsed light) rather than true laser technology—which makes them safer for you to use on your own, says dermatologist Julie Russak, MD.

Yes, at-home laser-hair removal devices do work, but they take consistency. Because at-home devices use lower energy levels than in-office treatments, they require continued use (think: weekly or every time you shave) to notice similar results, says cosmetic doctor Ana Mansouri, MD. Basically, they are not for the lazy.

No, laser hair removal isn’t necessarily permanent (even though that’s what they told us). Despite how they sound, these devices don’t permanently remove hair in a few sessions, leaving you hairless like a full-body wax. Instead, laser-removal devices only help to reduce the total number of hair follicles in your skin and potentially make your existing hair finer and thinner (only electrolysis hair removal can permanently remove all hair, FYI). Even in-office laser hair removal isn’t completely permanent. Typically, you’ll need to go in once every 8 to 12 weeks after you’ve done your round of treatments (which is usually between 6 to 12, depending on the area, your hair, etc.)

Neither IPL nor laser-hair removal is “better”—it really just comes down to preference. True laser hair removal only uses one wavelength of light (essentially, one precise laser beam) that targets just the hair follicle to help slow down hair growth. IPL (intense pulsed light), on the other hand, isn’t actually a laser. Instead, they emit broad-spectrum light in multiple wavelengths that spread across a larger area of skin, meaning they are less precise and won’t penetrate your skin as deeply. That’s why IPL devices are generally gentler and easier for people to use, which is why most at-home devices use IPL rather than laser beams.

That said, both IPL and laser-hair removal can slowly destroy your hair follicle to reduce hair growth over time with continued use. But expect laser-hair removal to be a bit stronger (and potentially more painful or irritating), which is why it often yields quicker and better results. Meanwhile, IPL devices are usually not as intense, so they’re great for using consistently at home or for use on sensitive skin, like on your bikini line or face.

Do dermatologists recommend at-home laser hair removal?

Yes, it’s okay to do laser-hair removal at home, according to Dr. Akhavan, but there are some things to consider. “At-home devices tend to be generally safe, though a real potential for adverse effects, like scarring, does exist,” he says. Which is why most dermatologists will suggest opting for the professional in-office route, if you have the means (laser hair removal can cost anywhere from $200 to $1,500 and usually requires at least six sessions spaced six weeks apart).

Just note: “If you’re using an at-home device, don’t use it on sensitive areas, like your face and neck,” says Dr. Akhavan. “And if you have sensitive skin, always patch-test first on a smaller area, then wait three to four weeks to make sure there are no complications.” And depending on the area you’re treating, you may also need to adjust your skincare routine to exclude skin-thinning ingredients (like chemical exfoliants or retinol serums) for three days before and after your laser-hair removal treatment.

Does at-home laser hair removal work on dark skin?

Currently, at-home laser-hair removal devices don’t truly work for dark skin. These lasers work by targeting the dark color of your hair follicle, and at-home devices don’t have the technology yet to properly distinguish between dark hair follicles and dark skin, meaning they can potentially burn you. While some devices might market themselves as being safe for dark skin, they are usually considering medium-to-tan skin versus actual deep-brown skin tones.

So far, there are no at-home devices that are safe to use on true type-six Fitzpatrick scale skin tones (reminder: This dermatological scale ranges from one to six, with six being “brown to dark-brown” skin). Most laser-hair removal devices are only approved for up to level-four skin tones, with four being “olive to medium” skin. But if you’re unsure where your skin tone lies, talk to your dermatologist before you try an at-home laser device.

What happens if you don’t shave before at home laser hair removal?

Yes, you should shave about 24 hours before using an at-home laser-hair removal device, otherwise the IPL/laser won’t be able to correctly target the melanin (pigment) in your hair follicle. This could potentially cause discomfort, like redness, swelling, and irritation. Because these devices work by homing in on dark, contrasting colors (think: a black dot on a white canvas), they need to be able to differentiate between your skin and your follicle to prevent errors or burns. FYI: Even stubble isn't okay for laser hair removal because it will create too much heat and can damage your skin (plus, it won’t even work to help reduce the hair anyway).

Just remember: Don’t wax before your laser hair removal. Waxing completely rips out the hair and its root, which will leave nothing for the light to target, and you won’t be able to use your device. Instead, make sure your skin is freshly shaved and free of lotions or creams.

At-home laser hair removal can be slightly painful for some, but it all depends on your pain tolerance. Many people describe it as a light sting—like a rubber band snapping against your skin—but some people don’t even notice any pain at all. Your device’s strength and intensity levels will also dictate how painful the treatment will be. In general, though, most people say it’s relatively comfortable when done at home (I would give it a 1 out of 10—it’s a piece of cake, IMO).

For the most part, yes, you can use at-home laser hair removal devices on your bikini line. But you should be very careful. This should only ever be done externally on your vulva. And keep in mind how sensitive the skin in your pubic region is. Generally, it's best to opt for the lightest setting, and go slow with the device. Of all of the at-home IPL devices we tested, our favorite for pubic hair was the NOOD Flasher Pro.

It's best to skip lasering until your skin has cleared if you're dealing with razor bumps. Reminder: Razor bumps are the tiny red bumps you get on your skin after shaving or waxing when the hair strand grows back into the skin instead of out. They're usually inflamed and irritated, which is why it's ideal to just let them heal and swipe on a soothing treatment for a few days before lasering.

If you’re just starting out with at-home laser hair removal, there can be a slight learning curve. Keep these in mind:

Like I said above, you have to shave beforehand to see any reduction in hair, and it’s usually recommended to shave right before. Usually, I’ll exfoliate in the shower the day before with a body scrub, then 24 hours later, I’ll shave and use my laser-hair removal device on clean, dry skin. Exfoliating the day before helps cut down on some irritation but still ensures a close shave.

While laser hair removal is typically well-tolerated, it can potentially make your skin a little inflamed and irritated. To offset this, always finish your treatments with a moisturizing body treatment, whether that’s a body lotion, body serum, or rich body butter. And if you’re prone to ingrown hairs, try incorporating an exfoliating treatment once or twice a week, like a chemical exfoliant pad or serum.

Remember: Laser hair removal takes time. One treatment won’t do much, and neither will a couple. It takes up to three months to start noticing a reduction in hair. Stay consistent (this usually means treating your skin once a week, but it depends on your skin type).

Only some devices are suitable for deep skin tones, and even fewer can work on light hair, so read the description of the product carefully, then scan the reviews for people who have a similar skin tone or hair color as you and see how they rate the device. We noted above if a device can be used on lighter hair, fyi.

You’ll also want to consider the size of the device you’re purchasing and the area that you intend to use it on, says Dr. Mansouri. Basically, a tiny device meant for your face probably isn’t going to be the best if you’re looking for a tool to quickly target your legs.

Do you want a device you can take on the go? Then you should definitely opt for something cordless (The only one we tested that we loved was by Tria Beauty, FYI). Or if you want something that’s going to be really easy to use, try the Braun Skin-I Expert, which connects to an app to help you track your treatments. Another loved feature by testers? A cooling effect to help bring down potential discomfort and pain, like the Ulike IPL device.

#6976. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

All three seasons have been fairly consisted, premiered in September or October of 2024, 2025, and 2026. If the show gets renewed, we can probably expect it in September or October of 2027.
