#6922. Is Your Home Too Matchy-Matchy? These 5 Designer Tricks Break Up the Blah and Add Instant Character

If your bedroom design began with a furniture set that matches your nightstands and lamps, it may be time to shake things up. Having a space where everything coordinates perfectly may seem like the easiest route to a stylish home, but many times, it can feel more sterile than a true reflection of who you are and what you love.

“A home often looks sterile when everything comes from the same set,” explains Preeti Shah, founder and principal designer at Spotlight Style.

By stepping out of your comfort zone and switching things up a bit, you can create a cohesive look that still brings character and depth to your home. Here are five designer tips to steer you in the right direction.

Just because one of your surfaces is natural white oak doesn't mean everything in the same room needs to follow suit.

“We love mixing dark wood tones with light tones and everything in between,” says Leah Hook, founder and principal designer at Gray Oak Studio.

Repeating wood tones can make a room feel cohesive, but you don't need to use the same tone everywhere. Think outside the box and look for subtle ways to tie the space together, such as repeating a wood tone in an art frame or decorative tray.

Believe it or not, your island does not have to match your kitchen countertops in every possible way.

“Choose a more exciting stone for your island. You can even miter it two inches thick instead of keeping everything the standard 1.25-inch thickness,” explains Gaelle Dudley, founder and creative director at GLDESIGN.

Dining chairs are almost always purchased as a matching set, but sourcing different styles from flea markets or Facebook Marketplace can give your dining room a fun, unexpected look.

“Select just one element from the table that will be repeated in the chairs, and go with something from a different collection or even an entirely different furniture line,” says Pattie Kelly, certified interior decorator and owner of Inspired Home Interiors.

Consider the height of the chairs (so that they still make sense together at the table) and how much room you have to pull them out in (so you’re not running into the wall behind them).

The most interesting homes display a mix of different art styles. Instead of sticking to one color palette or aesthetic, seek out pieces that contrast. For instance, Shah is a fan of contrasting graphic black-and-white drawings with gold-veined abstract canvases in various rooms of the home.

Speaking of artwork, as the space pictured above by Nina Garbiras illustrates, don’t be afraid to mix and match your frame materials. Just keep one element consistent, like the mat size or the frame width, so that it still has a curated (not chaotic) feel.

Just because you’re choosing one type of fixture finish doesn’t mean you have to repeat it everywhere. In fact, your space will look more “expensive” if you match shiny finishes very minimally.

For example, the above space by Vestige Home showcases how well brass and matte-black hardware can play off each other, adding depth, harmony, and visual interest. Choosing to go with one finish throughout this space would’ve become too matchy-matchy very quickly.

#6920. Will There Be Fewer Gowns at the Met Gala Next Year?

Announced today, the theme of the 2027 exhibition explores fashion’s obsession with everyday garments like T-shirts, jeans, and trench coats

Just over two months ago, the Met Costume Institute canceled its John Galliano retrospective, the original theme for the 2027 exhibition, amid donor and patrons outcry—today, it has announced the new direction, and it’s as far from Galliano’s fantastical fashions as possible.

The 2027 Costume exhibition and corresponding gala is titled “extraOrdinary: Fashion and the Poetics of the Everyday.” It will explore how designers reimagine quotidien ideas for the high-fashion world and how wearing, styling, and caring for these garments shapes their significance over time.

The exhibition will be divided into two larger themes. The first, “Familiar Forms,” will center archetypal garments like jeans, T-shirts, biker jackets, and trench coats, looking at how designers reinvent these pieces and play with the symbolism surrounding them. The other, “Common Matter,” will focus on disposable materials like paper, aluminum, and plastic, and how they have been elevated to fashion.

“The ordinary is never quite as simple as it appears,” said Andrew Bolton, Curator in Charge of the Costume Institute. “A pair of jeans, a white shirt, or a sweatshirt can hold traces of a life—the habits, memories, and attachments that make something our own. Designers work with that familiarity, revealing unexpected possibilities within forms and materials we think we already know. The exhibition invites us to discover how much imagination the everyday can hold, and how looking again might change what we value and care for.”

Bolton began where he left off last year with “Costume Art,” and the reality of placing the body at the center of your design process. He was particularly interested in moments like Jun Takahashi’s fall 2024 collection for Undercover, titled “Watching a Working Woman,” which was inspired in part by Wim Wenders’s 2023 film Perfect Days. He created ensembles that joined and layered familiar garments in new ways to subvert and complicate traditional perceptions of identity and form. The exhibition will open with pieces from this collection and expand to include over 200 items, including Matthieu Blazy’s trompe l’oeil leather jeans for Bottega Veneta, pieces from Marc Jacobs’s 1990s Perry Ellis grunge collection, Brooke Shields’s Calvin Klein jeans, Junya Watanabe’s leather biker jackets, Charles James’s 1930s couture puffer coat, and more.

The original theme, which would have centered on Galliano’s life and work, was initially announced in late July. After donors and critics spoke out against the designer’s past antisemitic and anti-Asian remarks, it was then recalled in late August. This decision was unprecedented; the Met had never made such a late-stage switch-up.

Galliano was known for his objectively extraordinary work subverting themes of historical excess and grandeur. His shows were more like theater productions. Now, it seems they’ve chosen a balm for the raucous in skewing in the complete opposite direction, promoting real life over fantasy. That said, this new theme is purported to examine familiar ideas through the extraordinary lens of fashion. There is certainly nothing ordinary about a Junya Watanabe biker jacket.

There is certainly much to explore here. The theme reminds us how much inspiration comes from the people walking down the street every day, the uniforms that have shaped our perceptions of society. Those ideas are ripe material for fashion designers’ creative minds —and can cause quite the stir among audiences. Just think of how shocked the industry was after Marc Jacobs elevated the way youth culture were dressing, what we call “grunge,” to the runway with his 1993 collection for Perry Ellis. Or the fascination with those optical illusion leather jeans Matthieu Blazy put on his Bottega Veneta runways a few years ago. After all, fashion is only fantastical relative to what we deem commonplace. We need a bit of the the familiar to help us digest something radical.

#6919. Here’s What It Takes to Keep a Pool Running During the Winter, According to Experts

Before you dive in, it’s important to understand the risks and expenses involved.

When most people think of backyard swimming pools, relaxing outdoors in the summertime immediately comes to mind. But some homeowners don’t want the swimming season to end when temperatures drop. Fans of cold-water swimming are starting to consider ways to enjoy their pools throughout the winter as a part of an at-home wellness experience.

“Ten years ago, most people didn’t think winter swims were possible, especially in colder climates,” says Karen Larson, cofounder of Soake Pools. “Times have changed because these days, they’re seeing neighbors, friends, and others do it and post the proof on social media.”

The increasing interest in cold-water experiences is part of a broader wellness trend. In American College of Sports Medicine's 2026 Worldwide Fitness Trends report, cold therapies, including cold plunges, cryotherapy, and cold showers, earned the 17th spot among fitness trends for the year, up from number 20 in its 2025 report.

If you’ve previously closed your swimming pool around Labor Day but are interested in making cold-water swimming a part of your daily wellness routine, there’s a lot to keep in mind. Not every existing pool is a good candidate for keeping open for winter swimming, and it’s important to understand the risks and expenses involved. But if you’re curious, here’s what to consider about keeping a pool open year-round.

Why is cold-water swimming becoming more common? There are numerous purported mental and wellness benefits, from athletic recovery to stress relief and better sleep. A 2025 systematic review and meta-analysis published in PLOS ONE found that cold-water immersion led to a reduction in stress for 12 hours afterward as well as improvements in sleep quality and overall quality of life.

“Winter swimming often creates a time where friends and family can come together without distractions,” adds Larson.

That said, while swimming during the colder months has its perks, there are also several risks to keep in mind. A 2020 review in the International Journal of Environmental Research and Public Health found that cold-water immersion can trigger an immediate shock response, leading to involuntary gasping, hyperventilation, and a rapid increase in heart rate and blood pressure. These reactions can make it difficult to control your breathing and increase your risk of drowning. Cold temps can also impair your muscle strength and nerve function, making it challenging to swim safely.

It’s a good idea to acclimate to the cold water gradually and avoid prolonged exposure. You’ll also want to make sure there’s someone else home while you take your cold-water plunge in the pool.

In addition, if you have underlying conditions like heart disease, consult your doctor before all else, as the cold water may place additional strain on your heart.

If you’re trying to consider whether your pool is a good candidate to keep open during the colder months, these are the things to look into.

The cost of keeping a swimming pool open for winter swimming will depend on your location, size, desired water temperature, and the type of heater you use.

“Routine pool maintenance generally remains about the same year-round, although chemical consumption may decrease depending on how frequently the pool is used,” explains Jim Eisch, Jr., vice president of operations at Pinch A Penny Pool Patio Spa. “The biggest expense to consider in the winter is heating the pool.”

For this reason, prospective winter swimmers may want to consider an upgrade to their heating system. Instead of using pricey natural gas heating or a traditional heat pump, Eisch says that inverter technology is one of the most affordable ways to maintain a swimmable pool temperature year-round.

For example, if you live in Florida and have a 10,000-gallon swimming pool, Eisch estimates your annual heating costs will be $2,467 with a natural gas heater, $741 with a traditional heat pump, and $434 for a heat/cool pump that uses inverter technology.

As aforementioned, the goal with wintertime swimming is not to be soaking in bathwater-like temperatures while it’s freezing outside. For safety purposes, winter swims are quick, and the heating system is to keep your chlorinated pool water well above the 19-degree Fahrenheit freezing point.

Unsurprisingly, a standard 14-by-28-foot swimming pool is going to be one of the hardest to use effectively year-round, unless you live in a region with a mild wintertime climate.

“You can technically use a large pool year-round, but it’s difficult to cover all its surface area well enough to hold in the heat, so heating bills can climb fast,” says Larson.

Therefore, smaller sizes like a 10-by-16-foot cocktail pool or plunge pool are better candidates for keeping open. Dedicated winter swimmers should also invest in an easy on-and-off cover—or a motorized auto cover—for added protection.

Regular pool cleaning and maintenance are essential, and this upkeep over the winter is no exception.

“Check the filtration system regularly to make sure it’s circulating smoothly, monitor the water chemistry, and plan for power outages—because there’s a good chance they will happen,” Larson explains. For this reason, you should also invest in a generator that can keep the pump running if the power goes out.

Safe pool access is just as important as any other aspect of the pool’s design. That’s why a nonslip surface, excellent drainage, and ample lighting are key.

“Your design should also provide a clear path from the house to the water,” says Neha Gupta, interior designer and founder at NU Curations.

Especially in the wintertime when ice and snow are a factor in your region, make sure you have a safe and smooth exit plan for getting out of the water, bundled up, and into your warm home without any obstacles. And then, of course, remember to cover the pool back up to keep it protected and ready for your next plunge.

#6917. Meet Levi Alves McConaughey: Matthew McConaughey and Camila Alves’s Son

The 18-year-old is stepping into the spotlight

Is Levi Alves McConaughey Hollywood’s next rising star to know? Matthew McConaughey and Camila Alves’s eldest son turned 18 earlier this year and seems to be following in his father’s footsteps, gradually stepping into his own spotlight.

Over the years, Levi has joined his famous parents on red carpets and at industry events, most recently at last night’s Airbnb.Org Gala in Los Angeles, where he even had his own solo moment on the red carpet. With two acting credits, a runway debut, and several Fashion Week appearances under his belt, he’s beginning to build a public persona of his own. And in the world of celebrity offspring, all signs certainly point to a career in the making for Levi.

Here’s everything to know about the young McConaughey, who’s just getting started.

Matthew McConaughey and Camila Alves McConaughey welcomed their first child, Levi Alves McConaughey, on July 7, 2008. He’s the eldest of the Hollywood couple’s three children. They also share a daughter, Vida (born in 2010), and a son, Livingston (born in 2012).

Over the years, Matthew has spoken about the importance of instilling a strong work ethic in his three children. During a 2020 appearance at The History Channel’s HISTORYTalks, the actor called his kids his “living legacy” and said he taught them the value of hard work by giving them small jobs on his sets.

“I have my kids come out, we put them to work,” the Dazed and Confused star said. “We give them a little cash for catering.”

Levi has also joined his parents in giving back. In 2023, Alves brought Levi and daughter Vida to Refettorio Paris, where they helped cook and serve meals to people in need. Levi later reflected on the experience in an Instagram post, writing, “I got the chance to cook at @refettorioparis for some people that don’t normally get to have a really nice meal. Refettorio Paris serves first class meals to the homeless. They made a great restaurant with high-end chefs to cook—using produce that was going to be thrown away.”

He added, “It was really cool to serve some people that don’t normally get to have it.”

Levi made his acting debut in the 2025 film The Lost Bus, starring opposite his father. The up-and-coming actor plays Shaun McKay, the son of Kevin McKay, who is portrayed by his real-life dad.

But as he tried out for the role. Levi didn’t want his famous last name to give him a leg up. In an interview with People, the father-son duo revealed that Levi auditioned for the role anonymously, with his last name removed so he could earn the part all on his own.

“Can you pull the last name?” Matthew asked the film’s casting director. “She goes, ‘Yeah.’ And I got a call back and she said, ‘Our director Paul Greengrass said, ‘That’s the kid. That’s the son, Levi.’ And then she goes, and ‘I told him that’s actually Matthew’s son in real life.’”

For Levi, the role was an opportunity to explore his interest in acting. “This role wasn’t me going, ‘Oh, I’m going to become an actor,’ It was me saying, ‘I’m really interested in this.’”

Levi has entered the fashion scene on a few occasions, including sitting front row at the Dior Homme Spring 2026 show during Paris Fashion Week.

He also made his official catwalk debut during Milan Fashion Week this season, walking the Vogue World runway.

In 2025, Levi opened up to Interview magazine about his mom, dad, and budding Hollywood career. The teen shared why he idolizes both of his parents: “My dad for the man he is, because of the work he has been able to create, and how he’s handled it all,” he said. “My mom for the woman she is, her patience, and her want to help others.”

When asked if he wanted to be famous, he said, “Famous in a lasting way. I want to continue to build on a legacy that I have the privilege to carry on.”

#6916. 10 Stylish Sofa Covers to Totally Refresh Your Living Room Before the Holidays

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Hide stains, protect upholstery, and add instant polish.

Life at home gets messy with its regular residents, and it gets even crazier when guests come over. Hosting season demands the most of our couches, as they become extra seats for family dinner or makeshift beds for overnight stays. It’s not always feasible to swap out your sofa just because the upholstery has seen better days. That’s where the mighty sofa cover comes in.

Whether you opt for a tightly-fitted silhouette or a looser, blanket-style slipcover, a high-quality sofa cover can totally refresh your space. Just think about it: a dated, yellowing couch suddenly looks fresh and brand new. You can even go bold and experiment with patterned options, without the commitment of buying a whole new piece. Pro tip: patterns are also a great way to hide stains.

There are hundreds of sofa cover options available, so below, our shopping team did the hard part for you. We hunted down nine options for different types of couches, including an editor-tested and approved option. Plus, we chatted with interior designer Tiffany Leigh for tips on styling sofa covers, keeping them clean, and more.

Velvet couches are having a moment, so if you want in on the trend but don't want to toss out your current sofa, enter this sumptuous sofa slipcover. Stretch the base piece to fit around a sofa measuring 72 to 96 inches, then cover three cushions with the included covers. "Even better: it was so soft against my skin that I fell asleep on it during movie night more times than I'm proud of."

This blanket-style cover drapes easily over a two-three cushion couch, and it’s made of a non-slip fabric designed to stay in place. Even with the rowdiest kids and pets.

You can protect the chaise arm with ease, and it’s still stain-resistant and machine-washable.

The size of your sofa cover will depend not only on the size of the sofa or couch you want to protect, but also on the properties of the fabric itself. Leigh says that with a stretchy fabric, you may want a cover with measurements slightly smaller than the dimensions of your sofa for a tighter, more fitted look. For sofa covers with corner ties that allow you to tighten or tie bows, the fabric may have a bit less give, so you'll want to allow for a little extra room in the cover size.

Some styles lend themselves to being covered more effectively than others. Leigh says that sofas with thinner or rolled arms tend to look nicer with a slipcover than those without. You can also choose between having a skirt on your sofa cover or showing off the legs of your furniture, depending on your aesthetic preference. “I like when they go right to the floor because a skirt cover is a much more traditional, covered look,” Leigh says. “If you have a little bit more of a modern style, having a slipcover that stops at the legs can be a fresher, kind of more updated take.”

Natural fabrics, such as chenille or linen, are typically a great choice when selecting a sofa cover fabric. For family rooms or homes with pets, indoor/outdoor fabrics like Crypton that have been treated to repel dirt and debris are safer bets for those wanting something stain-resistant. The stretchiness of the fabric also plays a role in selecting a sofa cover. "If you wanted something a little bit more durable, canvas or cotton would be a really great material on there," Leigh says.

It's always a good idea to pay attention to the care instructions for whatever cover you buy, as fabric care really depends on the individual material. For example, a heavier cotton material can likely withstand more washing. “I would say only wash when you really need to, just like with a really nice sweater,” Leigh says. “Don't wash it every time you wear it because washing breaks down fibers. So, it could reduce the longevity of your sofa cover. Most are designed to be removed and washed. So if and when you feel like 'oh, you know what, it's looking a little bit dingy, it's time,' that's the time to do it.” Additionally, it's essential to consider the size of your cover, as larger ones may necessitate the use of a commercial washing machine.

Additionally, the cover may wrinkle over time, especially with linen, which softens with each wash. “Linen does get a little bit wrinkly, but I think that's kind of the charm of a slipcovered sofa, having that kind of rumpled, you know, 'someone just took a nap here' look,” Leigh says.

“An oatmeal linen slipcover is that very quintessential, relaxed coastal style that you see a lot of slipcovers used in, and I think it's a classic choice that you'll never go wrong with,” Leigh says. Don't run away from patterns either, though—Leigh is seeing a rise in patterned upholstery in the design industry, and it's a chance to push the envelope and have fun with your design choices. “A cover can be a great way to, you know, try out a pattern without necessarily committing to it forever, because if in five years you say, ‘okay, this pattern was fun, but I'm ready for something different,’ you can just change the slipcover as opposed to having to get an all-new sofa,” Leigh says.

At House Beautiful, we are experts in reviewing and buying anything for the home, from mattresses for back pain to reclining sofas. For this story in particular, we asked our staff members to share their favorite sofa cover (if they own one) and spent hours combing the internet, reading hundreds of reviews about the best ones currently available on the market. While we haven't personally tested every product on this list, we've thoroughly outlined the pros and cons of each sofa cover to provide you with all the necessary details before you make a purchase.

For further insight into how to best place sofa covers in your home, we consulted interior designer Tiffany Leigh. Leigh runs her eponymous firm in Ontario, Canada, where her team helps clients bring their dream homes and cottages to life. Since 2019, they have designed approachable and livable interiors that feel welcoming and elevated, focusing on quality craftsmanship, emotional connection, and natural elements.

#6909. Pierpaolo Piccioli Is Stepping Down From Balenciaga

After just 16 months, the creative director and the brand are parting ways

After less than two years, Pierpaolo Piccioli is stepping down from Balenciaga, where he had been tasked with interpreting the house’s legacy of sculptural elegance without alienating the streetwear superfans his predecessor, Demna, courted.

Piccioli entered Balenciaga amidst the creative director roundabout of last year, a moment when many major fashion brands shuffled leadership. His debut, shown in the spring 2026 season, explored Balenciaga’s signature sack silhouette with simple evening dresses and separates alongside sculpted leather outerwear and bubble-hemmed skirts—all styled with space-age sunglasses and long leather gloves.

Throughout subsequent collections, he continued to chase an idea of pragmatic beauty. For Spring 2027, he showed elevated, voluminous versions of familiar ensembles like a T-shirt and jeans. Through that tenure, he courted high-profile fans like Meghan Markle and Tessa Thompson.

Born in 1967 in Italy, Piccioli studied fashion at the Istituto Europeo di Design in Rome. He interned at Brunello Cuccinelli before joining the team at Fendi, working alongside his design school friend Maria Grazia Chiuri, who would become his long-term creative partner. After roughly a decade, the pair went to Valentino to lead the accessories department in 1999; they were appointed co-creative directors of the brand in 2008. In 2016, Chiuri left to helm Dior, leaving Piccioli to lead the brand on his own.

There, he really established his legacy as a beacon of elegance and a maestro with color. Each season, he offered a new palette that encouraged audiences to rethink the rainbow—and how to pair its individual shades. His fall 2022 collection was rendered almost entirely in shocking pink, earning him a Pantone shade dubbed “Pink PP.” His couture collections in particular were celebrations of fashion, often eliciting tears from the audience—most notably from vocal fan Celine Dion. Yes, they were fantastical, but just wearable enough to keep shoppers returning to stores. After roughly 25 years at Valentino, he departed in 2024—then went to Balenciaga in 2025.

Cristóbal Balenciaga founded his namesake label in 1917 in San Sebastian, Spain, reestablishing his couture house in Paris in 1937. Often referred to as an “architect” of fashion rather than a designer, he is remembered for a vision rooted in shape above all. He cast the corsetry of the time aside and dealt instead in the manipulation of volume and silhouette. His creations were both sculptural and fluid, with iconic shapes including the “tunic” and “sack” silhouettes. His legacy was carried on by subsequent directors Michel Goma, Alexander Wang, and most notably, Nicolas Ghesquière and then Demna.

In theory, it could have been a great match for Piccioli. But Demna, who helmed Balenciaga from 2015 to 2025, turned those founding principles upside down. Unlike Cristóbal, he designed with satire top of mind. The designer injected his singular vision from his very early days on the job, creating collections that blended the classic codes of Cristóbal with his own signature high-low, European club-kid aesthetic. In doing so, he transformed the French fashion house into a mega-brand beloved by a spectrum of superfans across both menswear and womenswear. And that is a difficult consumer base to turn away from.

Piccioli had what was undeniably a difficult job, one rather symptomatic of the current state of fashion in which creative-director shakeups have become so commonplace. Three seasons is a very short time to establish a new look for a fashion brand, especially a sales behemoth like Balenciaga. Piccioli is immensely talented, and we look forward to whatever he has in store for us next.

#6908. This 100-Year-Old Louisiana Home Got a New Plot of Land and a Colorful New Chapter

The expansive property also includes a pool house and carriage house.

Long before it became the backdrop for family dinners, afternoon swims, and spirited tennis matches, this century-old Colonial in Baton Rouge, Louisiana, had already lived a rather extraordinary life. In the late 1920s, the house was dismantled and relocated from Prairieville to its current address, where its distinctive red clay roof tiles—salvaged from Louisiana State University—have presided over a landscape of majestic live oaks ever since. Decades later, a young girl growing up down the street would admire the house, never imagining that one day she’d raise a family of her own beneath that very roof.

When she and her husband finally purchased the property, they knew that turning a beloved neighborhood landmark into a forever home for themselves and their three daughters would require a little imagination—and a lot of careful planning. Enter interior designer Anne Underwood, who was tasked with bringing the historic residence into the present while preserving the quirks that made it so beloved. Over several years, the project grew from a renovation of the original four-bedroom home into a sprawling family compound, complete with a carriage house, pool house, and landscaped grounds designed to make an ordinary weekend feel like a vacation.

Inside, Underwood embraced the home’s traditional bones without letting them stiffen the home (and the family’s) sunny disposition. Lemon-yellow checkerboard floors mingle with antique furnishings (an element Underwood says is a must for creating “a lived-in, collected-over-time feel that I find crucial to making a true home”), hand-painted finishes, and exuberant textiles. A whimsical chandelier welcomes guests into the living room, while a tucked-away banquette has become an unofficial headquarters for homework, puzzles, and art projects.

Outside, the property’s newest additions—including a pool house and carriage house—offer an equally playful proposition. Conceived as the estate’s crown jewel, the pool house features pale wood walls, a colorful handwoven light fixture, and a monumental wood-burning pizza oven. The homeowners’ relatives jokingly dubbed the expanding property their own private resort, a description Underwood happily embraces.

“So much was poured into this project to make it unique, joyful, and really functional for the clients,” says Underwood. “The warmth and care of each detailed layer creates a sense of ease, which is what facilitates the joyfulness that plays out inside and outside these walls.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne UnderwoodArchitect: LRK ArchitectsContractor: Bernhardt Normand ConstructionStyling: Lucy BammanLandscape Architect: ARBO Landscape Architects Location: Baton Rouge, LouisianaThe Space: A four-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom main house, with a carriage house and pool house.

With a simpler architectural structure, the pool house was a place where Underwood leaned into the family’s playful energy. “We selected the wall stain to look like the inside of a shell and wanted the furnishings to feel layered, colorful, vibrant—like a vacation but also very durable for wet swimsuits and sweaty tennis players!” she adds.

The first selection for the pool house was also its most exuberant: a 12-light PET Lamp fixture, with each colorful pendant and cord individually customized and positioned beneath the soaring vaulted ceiling. “Its colorful, playful nature was exactly what we wanted and is very effective for balancing light throughout the space with high vaulted ceilings,” says Underwood. Below, a generous sectional upholstered in indoor-outdoor fabric creates an inviting place to retreat after a swim.

With vibrant Moroccan mosaic flooring, blue zellige shower tile, and a vintage wooden mirror, the pool house bathroom could easily belong to a boutique hotel on some far-flung island. Wood siding and thoughtfully chosen antique details lend the space a relaxed, resort-inspired character that feels right at home beside the pool.

Tradition gets a playful twist in the home’s primary gathering space, where a hand-knotted rug and classic upholstered furnishings mingle with an exuberant chandelier by Stray Dog Designs.

“This house lacks a foyer, so we wanted the living room to be welcoming with plenty of seating and layers of color and accessories,” says Underwood. “While this is the more ‘formal’ living space, we selected a bright, whimsical light fixture to let guests know that these homeowners don’t take themselves (or their interiors) too seriously.”

A passageway between the living room and the home’s more casual quarters, the dining room needed to make an impression without feeling overly precious. Schumacher’s Weeping Pine wallpaper wraps the space in pattern, while pale blue trim, upholstered chairs, and vintage sconces sourced from Round Top lend the formal setting an unexpected sense of ease.

With the adjoining breakfast room to consider, Underwood gave the kitchen a quieter disposition. Existing cabinetry received a custom pale green stain, expertly applied by a local faux-finish artist, while paneled appliances and tonal counter stools create a seamless effect. The yellow-and-white checkerboard flooring continues underfoot, connecting the two spaces.

A powder room is as good a place as any to indulge a dramatic streak. “We wanted a bold escape for guests in the powder bath, and this incredible grasscloth wallpaper was the jumping-off point,” says Underwood. She complemented the Aux Abris pattern’s lush, atmospheric tone with a wood-grained antique chest repurposed as a marble-topped vanity. Vintage Murano glass sconces and a striped inlay mirror add a final dose of eccentricity.

In the office, Underwood leaned into the appeal of a well-appointed study, furnishing this hardworking space almost entirely with vintage and antique finds. Custom cabinetry painted in Benjamin Moore’s Templeton Gray conceals printers, files, and other less-than-picturesque necessities, while mohair-upholstered armchairs make the space equally suited to work and contemplation.

As it turns out, the inspiration for the primary suite was waiting just outside the window. “I started with the idea of bringing materials—like the patinated copper on all exterior lanterns and gutters—from the exterior to the interior,” says Underwood. “While the walls are painted a green/blue color reminiscent of the patina of weathered copper, the accents in the room are warm shades of camel and copper.”

A Gustavian antique secretary and dimensional papier-maché art from Gusto complete the room's quietly collected atmosphere.

The generously proportioned soaking tub in the primary bathroom has already lived several lives, first as a favorite bathing spot for the family’s three young daughters, and now as a welcome retreat for their parents. Underwood carried the bedroom’s blue-green palette into the bath, introducing blush-patterned wallpaper, graphic stone tile, and black accents to balance the room’s softer flourishes.

In daughter Emily’s room, Underwood chose to embrace the sloped ceiling, outfitting it with custom bed canopies that create an enveloping, almost storybook atmosphere. Layers of patterned bedding, including bespoke eiderdowns made in the UK, heighten the cozy effect, while chunky upholstered ottomans offer a welcome counterpoint to the room’s more feminine details.

Making sense of the home’s quirky upstairs architecture required some clever spatial maneuvering. Underwood divided one large bathroom into two separate spaces, each with its own dressing vanity. In daughter Olivia’s bath, Salvesen Graham floral wallpaper covers the sloped walls and ceiling, visually softening the room’s unusual angles. “The pattern play in the tile, wallpaper, and fabrics keeps the energy moving throughout the space,” she adds.

In the carriage house, Underwood continued the layered approach with velvet, chenille, and a generously proportioned sectional, creating a retreat where overnight guests can comfortably settle in for an afternoon—or an entire weekend.

The carriage house kitchen offers guests all the comforts of home, with a nostalgic twist. Cabinetry painted Benjamin Moore’s Mistletoe features vintage-inspired vent detailing, while a retro SMEG refrigerator reinforces the room’s charming, slightly old-fashioned character.

Underwood preserved the home’s original red clay roof tiles while refreshing its facade using Benjamin Moore’s White Dov on the siding and Kennebunkport Green on the shutters. An expanded bluestone sidewalk gives the front door prominence while mature live oaks lend the property a sense of permanence that no renovation could replicate.

Anne Underwood is the founder of Underwood Interiors. Known for her thoughtful approach to creating layered, Anne creates livable spaces that balance timeless tradition with a fresh, playful sensibility. With an appreciation for antiques, vintage furnishings, and artisanal craftsmanship, her interiors feel collected over time rather than decorated all at once. Her work celebrates color, pattern, and architectural character, with an emphasis on thoughtful details and everyday functionality. Whether reimagining a historic residence or designing a new space from the ground up, she believes the most successful interiors reflect the personalities of the people who call them home.

#6900. Prince Harry and Prince William Pen Rare Letters About Their Mother to the Winners of the Diana Award

The prize recognizes “young people who work to improve the lives of others”

One area in which Prince Harry and Prince William will always share common ground is in their respect for their late mother, Princess Diana.

Two years after Diana’s untimely passing in 1997, the Diana Award was established by former U.K. Prime Minister Gordon Brown, in an effort to recognize young people who “work to improve the lives of others.” Now, the 2026 recipients have received a special treat in the form of personalized letters from Harry and William, who expressed their congratulations, while recognizing that the winners are helping keep Diana’s legacy alive.

“You should be immensely proud of what you have achieved and the difference you are making in your community and beyond,” Harry wrote in his message. “This award is deeply personal to me, not only because it carries my mother’s name, but because it champions her belief that young people have the power to change the world. You are putting that belief into action and inspiring others in the process.”

Meanwhile, William noted: “The world we live in today can feel challenging and uncertain, and creating change is not easy. Yet, in the face of these challenges, you have shown extraordinary resilience, courage, and a belief in the power of kindness to make things better.”

The Prince of Wales also added: “My mother, in whose memory you receive the Diana Award today, would be so proud of your achievements and the deep care you show for others. Thank you for your compassionate leadership, which is helping to create a more hopeful and positive world.”

Over the past few years, the royal brothers have continued their tradition of honoring Diana Award recipients. Back in 2023, the Duke of Sussex even made an in-person visit to see some of the honorees at a Conversations for Change seminar in Los Angeles.

However, most of their efforts have been done separately, as the two continue to deal with strains in their relationship.

One of the last times the princes were seen together was back in 2021, when they attended the unveiling of a statue dedicated to their mother at the Sunken Garden in Kensington Palace. The ceremony was held on what would have been Diana’s 60th birthday.

#6896. Your Houseplants Need a New Care Routine for Winter—Here Are 4 Easy Habits to Start

Give your indoor greenery a fighting chance until spring.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

With colder weather drawing our attention indoors, many of us swap outdoor garden maintenance for caring for our favorite houseplants. But while your spider plant, peace lily, or monstera may be safely tucked away inside, their needs change significantly during autumn and winter.

Reduced daylight, cooler temperatures, and drier indoor air can all affect the health of houseplants, meaning the care routine that worked perfectly in summer could actually do more harm than good during the colder months.

It turns out many of us are unknowingly making a few common mistakes that can leave indoor plants struggling through winter. Here are the habits to avoid, plus the simple swaps that will help keep your houseplants healthy until spring.

You might assume that moving your houseplant closer to a bright window is an excellent idea in winter to allow it to drink in as much sunlight as possible. And while this isn't necessarily a bad move, it just requires a little more thought.

“It’s always a good idea to move them closer to a bright window as darker days hit, but make sure to avoid cold drafts and radiators,” Mark Sage, B&Q's horticulture expert, tells us. “Cold drafts and dry air from radiators can all take their toll on indoor plants, leading to yellowing leaves, rotten roots or even plant fatality.”

Similarly, Sage suggests thinking about where the sun actually shines in your home throughout autumn and winter.

“Where natural light may be limited, consider investing in a plant light to give an extra boost, while rotating larger plants daily can help avoid leggy, awkward growth as they stretch out looking for the sunshine,” he suggests.

When houseplants start to look a little worse for wear and growth begins to slow over winter, it's tempting to step up your care routine. You might find yourself watering more often, tending to leaves more regularly, or even feeding plants in an attempt to encourage new growth.

However, Sage warns that this is often the wrong approach. In fact, during autumn and winter, “sometimes the best thing you can do for your plants is leave them alone!”

He explains: “In a slower growth period, plants generally need less water and feeding, and winter certainly isn’t the time for regular repotting.”

Instead, Sage recommends letting the soil dry out between waterings to help prevent root rot and giving plants time to rest. “It's worth giving them a boost of plant feed ahead of the winter months, but a little goes a long way,” he says. “Give plants their final dose at the end of autumn.”

During summer, when houseplants are actively growing, it's common to spread them throughout your home so you can enjoy a touch of greenery in different rooms. However, according to Sage, this can work against them once temperatures drop.

Instead, he recommends bringing your plants together during autumn and winter to help recreate the conditions many tropical houseplants naturally prefer.

“Give your houseplants a helping hand through winter by clustering them together to create a more humid microclimate, as many of these plants originally come from much hotter parts of the world,” he recommends. “Grouping several varieties close together can help maintain humidity around them, which can be particularly useful when central heating makes indoor air drier.”

While watering may have been your main plant-care task during summer, it's a mistake to neglect cleaning your houseplants over autumn and winter. Just like the rest of your home, plants can gather dust, and that build-up can make it harder for them to make the most of limited daylight.

“During winter, leaves can gather a layer of dust,” says Sage. “Give them a wipe down every one to two weeks with a microfibre cloth to support photosynthesis, and to help them benefit from even the smallest burst of sunlight.”

In fact, Sage suggests combining cleaning and watering occasionally by giving your plants a gentle shower.

“Use lukewarm water to boost humidity and get rid of any pests, but keep an eye on the temperature as if it is too cold you can shock the roots, but too warm and you can scald the leaves.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6892. Jennifer Lawrence and Kim Kardashian’s NYC Night Out Adds to Their Quirky 11-Year History

Jennifer Lawrence and Kim Kardashian enjoyed a night out together in New York City yesterday, beginning the evening in regular attire and then ending it in matching Skims pajamas and robes as they left the Four Seasons hotel. At first glance, the pairing might seem unexpected, but Lawrence’s history with the Kardashian family goes back for over 10 years.

In 2023, Lawrence told Kylie Jenner while interviewing her for Interview magazine that she started following the family’s original reality show Keeping Up With the Kardashians when she was a teenager: “I’ve been watching the show since I was, not to date myself, 14, so I would say I know everything about your family,” she shared.

Her first big public moment with the Kardashian-Jenner family was in 2015. Kardashian’s mother, Kris Jenner, posted a photo of Lawrence in bed with her along with a birthday message.

In November 2017, Lawrence interviewed Kardashian on Jimmy Kimmel Live! while serving as a guest host for Jimmy Kimmel.

Kardashian revealed where they first met. “We were at a hotel in New York, and I don’t know where we were coming from, but it was like, 8, and we were going up to see this Axel Vervoordt suite at the Greenwich [Hotel] and I’m getting in the elevator with Kanye [West] and the doors are about to close and I hear Jennifer screaming, ‘I love your show!’ so loud across the whole lobby,” she recalled.

They also discussed Lawrence having dinner at Kris’s house two weeks before their interview and Kardashian dressing her up.

It marked their first significant public conversation:

Less than a year later, Lawrence quipped during a February 2018 event at The Wing that her friendship with Kardashian doesn’t go both ways. “I don’t know that she’d call me her friend,” Lawrence said. “It’s probably a one-sided friendship.”

She recalled learning she’d interview Kardashian on Kimmel’s show. “When I found out that I was going to be interviewing her, I was like, ‘Oh my God, my whole life has been building up to this moment,’” she said. “I wanted to get as much information as I could. I wanted to do my due diligence on Kim Kardashian. I went over to Kris [Jenner’s] house and had dinner with the whole family.”

Of the family generally, Lawrence said, “They are smart, grounded and normal and funny. They’re very nice people, [they’re] very close, [they have a] loyal bond with one another, which I think is a positive thing to put out into the world. They’ve been absolutely lovely to me.”

Lawrence has remained close to the Kardashian-Jenner family in the years since. (Kris even attended Lawrence’s wedding to Cooke Maroney in October 2019.)

During her 2023 conversation with Kylie, Lawrence even jokingly referred to Kris as her mother while asking a question about Kylie’s relationship with her older sister Kendall Jenner. “You and me are Leos,” she told Kylie. “We’re happy, excited golden retrievers. And Kendall, of course, is a Scorpio like our mother, Kris.” She and Kylie ended their chat by exchanging I love yous.

Last month, Lawrence was mentioned on The Kardashians, with Kardashian revealing the actress had asked for Kourtney Kardashian’s contact information to say sorry for a remark she made during a Vanity Fair interview about Kourtney.

Kardashian said that Lawrence wrote in her apology email, “I just feel like I’m one of your sisters.”

#6891. Presidents, Bullfighters, and California—Elaine de Kooning Painted Whatever She Wanted

And you can see it all at a new retrospective at Berry Campbell gallery in New York

In the history books, Elaine de Kooning is often eclipsed by her husband, the famed painter Willem. This fate befell the women in the Abstract Expressionist movement, including Lee Krasner, Grace Hartigan, Joan Mitchell, and Helen Frankenthaler, who became known as the “Ninth Street Women,” all of whom received their flowers after decades of being overshadowed by their male counterparts. But you simply cannot tell the story of mid-century art—and Abstract Expressionism—without Elaine.

De Kooning, who died in 1989 at 70, sat at the epicenter of the art world for five decades. She deftly balanced two parallel practices in portraiture and abstract expressionism, unafraid to allow these genres to intermingle. Beyond her painting, she founded the artist collective The Club in Greenwich Village and wrote art criticism for Art News, celebrating artists like Arshile Gorky and Jackson Pollock. President John F. Kennedy, New York artist Toni Ross, former Art News editor-in-chief Thomas Hess, and American painter Alex Katz are among the artist’s impressive roster of sitters. A number of these portraits currently hang at New York’s Berry Campbell gallery. Each image frames its subject with expressive brushwork and vivid, bright colors. Among these works, most featuring starry figures, is a young boy in a cowboy hat, featured in the painting Jemp #1 (1963). This little kid is De Kooning’s nephew, and the reason the show exists.

Gallerists Martha Campbell and Christine Berry connected with Chris Luyckx, the nephew, who had preserved De Kooning’s paintings for decades and felt her work deserved renewed attention. By the time they met, roughly a decade had passed since De Kooning’s last notable show, a retrospective at the National Portrait Gallery in 2015, followed by smaller shows that focused on specific periods of her life. “By doing these individual shows, you miss the incredible throughline of her work from the late 1940s until the day she died in 1989,” says Campbell. “It goes with our ethos of always believing in the artist from the very beginning until the very end.” Berry Campbell’s show, simply titled “Elaine De Kooning” and on view through December 19, celebrates the artist in her entirety, featuring around 30 works sourced directly from the family’s collection

De Kooning’s oeuvre retains its wonder by evading classification, collapsing boundaries between portraiture and abstract expressionism to forge a distinct visual sensibility. Part of her practice fit firmly in the world of abstract art. Paintings like Untitled (1960) reveled in obscuring the world around her; here, she depicts New York’s verticality. “Elaine de Kooning was an abstract expressionist, but as a woman artist, she didn’t have dealers breathing down her neck saying, ‘Paint more of what we see as Abstract Expressionism,” Campbell says, noting that this provided the artist with wiggle room.

This should not overshadow her exceptional command of pure abstraction. The 11-foot-wide California (1961), a barrage of vivid colors underscoring De Kooning’s compositional mastery, sits at the very back of the gallery. Berry affirms “it is the most important piece in the exhibition,” noting that “there is no other painting of that scale or magnitude or from that time period on the market, because very other painting that is like that is in a museum collection.” Doubling down on its consequence, Campbell recalls, “There’s a great note that we found from [legendary art dealer] Joan Washburn that says that when she was her dealer, she said, ‘This is the best painting that Elaine de Kooning has ever painted.’”

Elements of her abstract work often defined her portraiture. Energetic bursts of bright paint consumed her subjects. Tom Hess (1963), for instance, features the titular journalist among flurries of orange, yellow, and red paint, leaving most of the canvas untouched. “She was an abstract expressionist, but within her own definition of what that was, so she used these abstract expressionist brushstrokes to glorify what she loved.”

De Kooning loved her subjects, occasionally obsessing over one topic and painting it repeatedly, ranging from bullfighting and baseball to the painter Aristodimos Kaldis and President Kennedy. This new show highlights all of these hyperfocused periods, including a standout from her paintings of the former president. Her connection to him started in 1962, when Kennedy sat for De Kooning in Palm Beach to complete a commission for the Harry S. Truman Presidential Library & Museum. The fixation intensified after the president’s assassination in 1963. JFK #7 (1963), on view at the gallery, is only one example of a year’s worth of works.

“She did get fixated on JFK, because she was painting him and then he was shot,” Berry explains. “That was pretty shocking. She painted him for a full year without stopping. People only focus on JFK. But she did portraits from the beginning, in the 1940s, all the way through. She did self-portraits, as well as portraits of her neighbors and her boss, Tom Hess. The portraits are a through line of her career.”

A sophisticated command of color is evident across De Kooning’s entire body of work. Bright colors clash in her abstract works, and in her portraiture, color builds texture. Perhaps most surprising, however, is that De Kooning often avoided looking at color when creating her images. For instance, she preferred to use black-and-white newspaper clippings as references for her basketball paintings, imbuing them with color guided by her personal sensibility. “Color is key to what she does,” Berry explains, pointing to the portrait of poet Frank Lima, a friend with a drug problem whom De Kooning renders in muted greens.

One painting, Bacchus #20-G (1983), an image of Jules Dalou’s The Triumph of Silenus painted six years before her death, epitomizes what’s so striking about De Kooning’s paintings. The lush painting features the sculpture’s twisted bodies, rendered in an opulent violet, with a backdrop of verdant green. It demonstrates her capacity to seamlessly balance portraiture and Abstract Expressionism, a testament to her unwavering devotion to her distinct visual language. This vision, after years on the fringe, finally receives the flower it deserves, alongside, rather than below, her husband.

#6885. 13 Best Deodorant for Men 2026, According to Dermatologists

Frankly, the best deodorant for men sells itself. It stinks to stink: Body odor is right up there with bad breath as a totally avoidable misfortune. But while deodorant might seem like an odd category to get overly psyched about, as a longtime grooming editor, I can confidently say we're living in a golden age of the underarm essential.

Unlike the synthetic and chalky ghosts of deodorant sticks past, today’s best deodorants are a a sea of sophisticated formulas, improved textures, better scents, and naturally derived ingredients that, yes, actually work. The only hard part is honing in on the best one for you. Price, feel, skin sensitivity, and your sweat and odor levels matter. So I tested a whole lot of deodorants to narrow the field to seven standouts, some new, some tried-and-true, plus a few more deeper cuts I also loved.

I also tapped trusted dermatologists, for some expert insights further down. Just one last quick note: We’re not talking antiperspirants here, which primarily rely on aluminum compounds to reduce sweat. If that’s what you’re after, I’ve got recommendations for those, too.

Now, are there more targeted, more sophisticated, and, well, chicer deodorants on this list? Absolutely. But at the end of the day, you want your deo to do three things: keep the stink at bay, smell good, and be easy to repurchase. Dove Men+Care hits every mark, which is why it's in every drugstore.

The price, of course, is great, but then there's the scent: a surprisingly sophisticated blend of bright bergamot and warm white amber that's fresh, subtly citrusy, and softly warm. It smells so much better than your typical underarm fare and is actually a little mood-boosting, as the packaging suggests. The aluminum-free, baking-soda-free formula features moisture-attracting ingredients, giving it a little skincare credibility, too. It also feels super smooth going on, and kept its scent for most of the day. No notes!

Hip grooming brand Church California's deodorant only launched two years ago, but for me, it's everything a niche, forward-thinking deodorant should be: Effective, made with almost entirely naturally derived ingredients, and housed in compostable, plastic-free packaging. The formula uses magnesium and organic tapioca starch for odor and moisture control, with coconut oil and shea butter adding a little skin-conditioning goodness. And that scent? Wonderfully herbaceous, thanks to an essential oil blend that smells like the actual outdoors.

Admittedly, the sturdy cardboard tube takes a little getting used to—you push the stick up from the bottom instead of twisting a dial. And the glide is certainly a little less smooth. But oh, does the formula hold up. Even hours later, I'd catch little whiffs of those lovely botanicals, and when the scent eventually faded, there was still no detectable B.O. For the environmentally conscious guy, it's a no-brainer, and for the aesthete, it looks retro cool sitting on your medicine cabinet shelf.

Ursa Major’s Hoppin’ Fresh stick is a modern legend in the deo space. Among us grooming geeks, this au naturel-meets-elevated formula changed the underarm game when it launched in 2015. It was one of those early artisanal step-ups from drugstore fare, and it earned the hype with thoughtfully curated, foraged-sounding ingredients, a subtle, natural-smelling citrus scent, and a skincare-centric stick that glides on smoothly.

It still holds up today, fighting odor, soothing skin, and absorbing excess moisture to keep you smelling fresh. For an aluminum-free deo, it also keeps me pretty dry, too. And even the packaging—a sleek, solid-blue tube—feels like a style upgrade. Scent wise, in winter Forest Fix is the way to go. But the original Hoppin' Fresh is always waiting in the wings once the warmer months come around.

I'm well aware that Old Spice has plenty of newer, more fragrance-centric variations, many with names like Swagger. But I've always preferred the tried-and-true stuff, and that blue stick, here in High Endurance Refresh, is about as iconic as deodorant gets. It's my old reliable, and one I always keep as a backup, whether I'm visiting family, staying overnight somewhere, or just need something I can count on in a pinch. This classic has aged remarkably well, because it simply works.

Then there's the nostalgia, which hits me every time I catch a whiff of that distinctively fresh-meets-medicinal aroma I've loved unironically for years. The formula isn't particularly fancy: science-y ingredients give the aluminum-free stick its smooth glide, while glycerin adds a little skin-conditioning benefit. Would I wear this for an Ironman competition? Probably not. But for everyday errands, workdays, and just about everything in between? Absolutely. At under $15 for a three-pack, it's also easily one of the best value options on this list.

When you think gel deodorant, you probably picture those old-school perforated dispensers with tons of holes. But Baxter of California's is a smooth, transparent gel stick that's still, in my book, among the best. Part of the appeal is the formula, which packs some impressive botanicals: witch hazel and tea tree oil help tackle odor-causing bacteria, while chamomile helps soothe skin. Add tried-and-true glycerin to the mix, and you've got a gel deodorant with some serious skincare cred, too.

Then there's the application itself. The clear gel glides on effortlessly, with a cool, crisp, almost medicinal feel that's especially refreshing during the warmer months. Better still, it won't leave white marks on your shirts, something I always appreciate with my go-to black T-shirts. Is it the most heavy-duty odor fighter on this list? Not quite. But for everyday wear, it keeps the funk at bay without feeling like you've applied much of anything.

I test a lot of deodorants, but few become permanent fixtures in my bathroom. So it's worth telling you that Freaks of Nature's Everyday Endurance has become my primary deodorant lately. The brand may be known best for sunscreen, but it knows its way around our underarms too. And much like its sunscreens, part of the appeal is the application. The lotion-like formula emerges through a clever F-shaped grate, and there's something oddly satisfying about watching it dispense before smoothing it onto your underarms.

Better still, it absorbs well without any gunky, sticky residue. Cocoa butter and sunflower oil give it a skin-conditioning quality that makes this one of the most soothing deodorants I've ever worn. It's premium-priced, sure, but between the standout application, exemplary performance, and skin-conditioning feel, it's worth every penny.

Gone are the days when deodorant had to smell like “Sports Ice Rush.” Today's best odor fighters offer sophisticated, subtle scents (so they don't compete with your cologne), and Salt & Stone remains one of the top examples, with deodorants that have actual scent pyramids—Saffron & Cedar, Black Rose & Oud, Bergamot & Hinoki, for example. All are nuanced, refined, and never too loud.

But it's not just about the scent. The formula neutralizes odor and absorbs excess moisture very well, while skincare-centric additions like niacinamide and shea butter help keep underarms comfortable. More importantly, in my experience, the fragrance doesn't devolve into some funky olfactory murk by day's end. You still smell like the scent you applied that morning, or at the very least, fresh. Are these the heaviest-duty or most affordable deodorants out there? Nope. But they deliver on fragrance and function, and that's a combination worth investing in.

Caldera Lab just launched its first deodorant, and given the brand's skincare chops, it's no surprise the formula reads like a serum: niacinamide, aloe vera, and hyaluronic acid soothe and hydrate; postbiotics and magnesium tackle odor; and arrowroot absorbs moisture. Stacked, indeed.

Can Aesop do any wrong? Not really, and its deodorant spray is another example of the botanical-meets-aromatic goodness the amber-hued brand does so well. It's the only spray on this list, and while its odor protection holds up well, a midday refresh is sometimes warranted—and honestly, part of the pleasure.

If your skin is extra sensitive, Nécessaire's entire fragrance-free lineup is exemplary, and its deodorant stick is no exception. It keeps odor impressively in check, is easy on sensitive underarms, and won't compete with your favorite cologne. It's also aluminum- and baking soda-free, dermatologist-tested, hypoallergenic, and even carries the National Eczema Association's Seal of Acceptance.

Cremo may be kings of shave cream, but it knows a thing or two about deodorant, too. The formulas are affordable, the odor protection holds up, and the scents smell so damn good. Take Palo Santo, with its warm, woody, slightly smoky aroma that feels far more sophisticated than its price suggests. Coconut oil and shea butter add the skin-conditioning touch you'd expect from a pricier stick. rant quietly in the background, look elsewhere—but if you like a lot of fragrance with your funk-fighting, Cremo delivers serious bang for the buck.

The Grey's culty, rightfully hyped deodorant is the best old-school-style roll-on I've ever tried. The unscented formula is so lightweight you almost have to check you're applying anything, but oh, you are, and there's a cooling tingle as it goes on. It's the most expensive deodorant on this list, but with its premium slim aluminum casing and impressively lightweight formula, it's an investment in barely-there odor-fighting care.

Malin+Goetz's Eucalyptus Deodorant is a stone-cold classic that helped elevate gel sticks and make them feel refreshingly natural. The scent is practically a trademark at this point, equal parts medicinal, herbal, and oh-so-subtle. But there's substance behind it: eucalyptus extract helps refresh skin, while odor-neutralizing citronellyl methylcrotonate keeps the funk at bay.

As a grooming editor, I've tried more sticks, sprays, gels, and roll-ons than I can count, from drugstore staples to niche newcomers. And while plenty have impressed me on first swipe, the real test is whether I keep reaching for them weeks, months, or even years later.

Here, I drew on firsthand testing, focusing on odor protection, scent, texture, application, skin comfort, and, of course, how well they hold up. I've worn these deodorants through warm and cool weather, long days, and everything in between. Some are favorites I've relied on for years, while others are newer that are now part of my medicine cabinet rotation. As I've said, the best deodorant sometimes depends on the occasion or even the season, so it's a good idea to have a few on hand if possible. At the end of the day, a deodorant needs to keep B.O. at bay, ideally absorb excess moisture, and feel comfortable. Some excel in certain areas more than others, but each one above warrants its spot among the best deodorants.

Here is some dermatologist advice on deodorants—the best ingredients to look for, plus some controversial ones, too—with insights from board-certified dermatologists Kiran Mian, Ryan B. Turner, and Heather Rogers.

The primary cause of body odor is “bad” bacteria pairing up with sweat. There are two ways to prevent the stink: Reduce perspiration in the area, and/or create an inhospitable environment for the bacteria.

These are the most common ingredients to seek out if you want to temper the proliferation of bacteria in your pits (and anywhere you’re particularly pungent).

This antimicrobial and anti-inflammatory mineral safely and effectively kills bacteria without irritating skin, says Mian.

Dr. Mian also endorses the non-irritating magnesium hydroxide, a naturally occurring compound that neutralizes bacteria without absorbing into the skin.

“The terpenes in tea tree oil make this ingredient naturally antibacterial and antifungal, helping to reduce body odor,” Turner says. “In its pure, concentrated form, it can be very irritating. It’s best to use a product that’s formulated with tea tree oil at low percentages. It’s still effective when diluted, and a little bit goes a very long way.”

This one is controversial to some. Most skin types will tolerate baking soda (sodium bicarbonate) just fine, but only trust reputable, well-reviewed brands who use it. “Baking soda has a very alkaline pH level, while that of the skin is more acidic,” explains Turner. “This disrupts the skin’s natural barrier, harming the good bacteria in our microbiome, and leaving our skin more susceptible to irritation and inflammation.” (We’ve included a few deodorants with baking soda below, plus many natural picks that explicitly exclude it.)

It’s easy to get these two confused, but they share a principle. “Prebiotics feed the good bacteria on our skin (bacteria that does not break down sweat releasing a foils odor), which then overpopulates those odor-producing bacteria,” explains Mian. “Probiotics are the good bacteria that will ideally populate the skin and overtake odor causing bacteria.” The names of these might be strange to you, like lactobacillus acidophilus or saccharomyces ferment. Chances are, a brand will proudly tout their pre- and probiotics, so look for that indication instead.

“These chemical exfoliating ingredients help resurface the skin, which also strips away bacteria as well as removes the dead skin that bacteria break down, which causes body odor,” says Turner. And Dr. Rogers echoes the love: “Using acids to exfoliate the skin in our armpits helps rebuild the acidic mantle [which is compromised with age], leading to better control of bacterial growth and odor. Many acids work great; glycolic, lactic acid and mandelic acid are my favorites. Salicylic acid, a beta hydroxy acid, has a higher chance of irritation.”

“Activated charcoal can help to absorb sweat and help to neutralize odors,” says Turner.

Arrowroot powder, tapioca starch, and cornstarch are popular ingredients in aluminum-free deodorants, helping absorb excess moisture and keep underarms feeling dry without blocking sweat production. Of tapioca starch, Mian says, “This non-irritating starch won’t clog sweat ducts like aluminum, which can cause breakouts.” (It's also terrific in anti-chafe products for similar moisture-absorbing benefits.) “Like tapioca starch, corn starch pulls moisture from the skin to leave a powdery feeling behind. Unless you have an allergy to these plants, they are safer to use,” says Turner. Both doctors particularly favor arrowroot powder as a naturally derived, generally well-tolerated ingredient for absorbing moisture.

Both help soak up surface moisture without blocking sweat production. Freaks of Nature's formula includes bentonite clay.

A naturally occurring, porous mineral powder that absorbs excess moisture and helps keep underarms dry. You'll find it in Ursa Major's deodorant.

Approach your deodorant purchase with the following considerations:

While it’s easy to celebrate a fully natural formula—and for good reason—keep in mind that not all natural ingredients are inherently good for your skin. Ingredients like baking soda and certain essential oils can cause irritation, particularly if your underarms are sensitive. Conversely, synthetic ingredients aren't automatically bad for your skin, either. “What’s more important is to consider the manufacturer of the products—are they reputable? Do they perform extensive testing? What are the ingredients used and from where are they sourced?” says Turner. That's sound advice whether you're shopping natural or synthetic. There's a reason dermatologists frequently recommend established brands like Dove: They have the resources to develop, test, and manufacture formulas at scale, often at a fraction of the price of more niche alternatives. Of course, that doesn't mean smaller brands aren't doing their homework, either. Finding the right deodorant still takes some trial and error, gents, but you can always start with our list.

If your underarms are easily irritated, start with the ingredient list. Baking soda, strong fragrances, essential oils, and certain acids can trigger irritation, though everyone's skin reacts differently. Look for skin-conditioning ingredients like shea butter, glycerin, and aloe, several of which you'll find in the deodorants featured above. Fragrance-free formulas are a smart starting point for sensitive skin, while unscented are worth considering, too—just know that some contain masking fragrances.

Deodorants come in all sorts of formats these days, from classic solid sticks and clear gels to sprays, roll-ons, lotions, and pastes. How they feel when you apply them matters as much as how well they work. Some glide on more smoothly, while others feel chalkier, thicker, or more moisturizing. Gels and roll-ons tend to feel lightweight and refreshing (great for warmer weather), while richer lotions and pastes feel more nourishing and suit the cooler months. Ultimately, the best deodorant feels comfortable, doesn't leave residue on your clothes, and fits your lifestyle.

Dr. Kiran Mian is a board-certified dermatologist at Hudson Dermatology and Laser Surgery in NYC, and volunteer faculty at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai University.

Dr. Ryan B. Turner is a board-certified dermatologist and founder of Turner Dermatology in NYC and the TRNR Skin skin care line.

Dr. Heather Rogers is a board-certified dermatologist and co-founder of Modern Dermatology in Seattle and the founder of the Doctor Rogers skin care line.

#6884. Martine Rose Is the New Creative Director of Balenciaga

An appointment the fashion world is eager to celebrate

Fashion fans rejoice: Martine Rose has officially been appointed creative director of Balenciaga following yesterday’s exit of Pierpaolo Piccioli. This appointment represents a welcome departure from the circulation of familiar names for these creative director roles and symbolizes a shift back to an edgier Balenciaga established by Demna. She begins November 16.

Rose was rumored to be up for the job when it became available after Demna’s departure to Gucci in 2025. And it’s a homecoming; when Demna was appointed to Balenciaga as creative director in 2015, he brought Rose on as a menswear consultant. In her three years working there, she helped to shape the look of his very distinctive vision.

Rose was born in South London in 1980 to a British Jamaican family. Her mother was a nurse and her father was an accountant. She spent her childhood around slightly older relatives deeply immersed in ’90s rave subcultures. The symbolically rich clothing choices shaped her early aesthetic way more than any Paris runway could. She studied fashion design at Middlesex University, where she discovered an affinity with menswear. Rose began creating early, opening the small T-shirt line LMNOP with a friend in 2002. She launched her namesake label in 2007 and began showing under the umbrella of Lulu Kennedy’s fashion incubator program Fashion East in the early 2010s. That’s where she began gaining traction in niche fashion circles.

With her own collection, Rose elevated her memories of clubs—and the clashes of the different tribes within them—for a fashion audience, offering revisions of those shiny shirts, baggy pants, and anoraks. She is known for subverting archetypal garments, distorting proportions, and invoking unconventional fabrics like satin and lace. Her work nods to subcultural heroes who used fashion to convey identity, like the Lo Lifes and Dapper Dan. She can shrink a pair of pants just enough to be a little awkward and pair them with a sports jersey and loafers with ultra-extended toes in an ensemble that somehow makes total sense. Through her lens, the mundane becomes complex, thought-provoking, even extraordinary.

Demna caught on to what Rose was doing and brought her on to develop Balenciaga menswear following his appointment in 2015. She worked with him for three years and helped influence his highly recognizable men’s aesthetic, one that often featured the exaggerated shoulders, boxy cuts, and logo experimentation that she already favored. Her work at the luxury house brought attention to her own label, buoying its renown in fashion. She has since produced several high-profile collabs with brands like Nike, Supreme, and Clarks, and she counts Timothée Chalamet, Rihanna, and Hailey Bieber among her devotees.

Balenciaga is having a bit of an identity crisis. The couture house was founded by Cristóbal Balenciaga in 1917 in San Sebastián, Spain, and later reestablished in Paris in 1937. Often referred to as an “architect” of fashion rather than a designer, Cristóbal is remembered for a vision rooted in shape, volume, and silhouette. His creations were both sculptural and fluid, with iconic looks including the “tunic” and “sack” silhouettes. His legacy was carried on by Michel Goma, Alexander Wang, and most notably, Nicolas Ghesquière and Demna.

In 2025, amid an industrywide reshuffling of creative directors, Balenciaga appointed Pierpaolo Piccioli to its helm, tasking him with interpreting the house’s legacy of sculptural elegance without alienating the streetwear superfans Demna courted. It was a difficult job; Piccioli left just 16 months later. Rose’s appointment shows that the house is looking to reignite some of Demna’s subversive edge. But zooming out, her return is undeniably a celebratory one; she is beloved by fashion and streetwear fans alike. It feels in alignment with the excitement around Grace Wales Bonner’s appointment to Hermès. The current roster of creative directors at big luxury houses is mostly white and male; these are two women of color whose work has been quietly shaping the fashion landscape without always getting their due.
