#6882. This Luxury Spa Is New England’s Best-Kept Secret

Powered by Valmont at The Preserve Sporting Club & Resort, the Hilltop Spa is Rhode Island’s hidden gem

Rhode Island may not be the first place you think of when booking a luxury spa and resort getaway, but it should be. While the Ocean State is well known for its miles of Atlantic coastline, an exclusive, if-you-know-you-know destination is tucked away among 3,500 acres of rolling hills: Richmond’s The Preserve Sporting Club & Resort.

The Preserve is designed for nature lovers, with hiking trails, on-site equestrian training stables, and the nation’s only North American Bentley off-road track (the resort even picked me up in a Bentley truck from the Providence train station). The grounds also offer a range of activities, like clay shooting, archery, rock climbing, ziplining, and more, depending on the season. But beyond the private sporting club and resort’s recreational charms, The Preserve is equally intentional about members’ and visitors’ self-care, one reason the resort partnered with Valmont Skincare to develop the beauty brand’s fourth spa location, the Hilltop Spa.

Valmont’s 41-year-old brand is steeped in Swiss scientific expertise, which I experienced during my weekend-long stay at The Preserve and my visit to the Hilltop Spa. Beyond steam rooms, saunas, and private whirlpools in both the women’s and men’s locker rooms, the spa features a coed hydrotherapy area with two whirlpools, a cold plunge, and a private after-dark experience complete with champagne.

After a quick change into a fluffy robe, I relaxed with a brief aromatherapy session, then received the Preserve Custom massage, which was deeply restorative. A few minutes later, after lounging in the spa’s designated relaxation room and enjoying some healthy snacks, I moved on to my customized Hydration of the Bisses facial, a plumping 60- to 90-minute treatment that targets the lymphatic system to improve fluid flow. No matter which Valmont treatment you choose at the Hilltop Spa, each includes its own Butterfly Motion, a proprietary massage technique.

“Valmont’s signature facials target main skin concerns like hydration, lifting, and firming, and combine cellular cosmetics with exclusive massage techniques,” says Viviana Garrido, Valmont’s U.S. sales director. “Guests love the immediate glow, lifting effect, and sense of deep relaxation. What makes the experience unique is the combination of performing yet relaxing protocols in an immersive sensory journey inspired by the Swiss Alps, delivering results for both the skin and the mind.”

True to The Preserve’s ethos of holistic beauty and wellness in the treatment room and beyond, the spa is often a first, though likely recurring, stop for its guests. Beyond its Valmont-approved amenities, the sporting club offers yoga, Pilates, sound bathing, and personalized fitness training, some of which I also took part in during my stay.

“Each provides an opportunity for guests to reconnect with themselves through movement, mindfulness, and relaxation,” notes resort manager Clinton Cox. To mentally disconnect in warmer months and through mid-fall, The Preserve also offers its bespoke Tree House Experience, developed by Cox himself and designed for couples, close friends, or small groups. The two-hour immersive forest wellness journey combines therapeutic body treatments, guided stretching, a 90-minute massage, relaxation, and a specially curated culinary menu.

“At The Preserve, we believe skincare should extend beyond the treatment room,” explains Cox. “It is not simply about how your skin looks when you leave the spa; it is about investing in its long-term health, vitality, and appearance while embracing an experience defined by exceptional service, refined surroundings, and genuine hospitality.”

Garrido agrees, noting that the company partnered with The Preserve as its fourth spa location in the United States because of shared philosophies of exceptional craftsmanship for guest and user experiences, a deep respect for nature, and a shared commitment to longevity. “Its unique setting and reputation for luxury hospitality provide the perfect environment for Valmont treatments and skincare expertise,” says Garrido. “The partnership allows us to offer guests a truly immersive wellness experience where luxury, performance, longevity, and well-being come together naturally.”

With so much to try, you can tailor a visit to The Preserve to your unique beauty and wellness preferences, which is part of what makes the Rhode Island sporting club and resort so singular. Seemingly off the beaten path, but more like hidden in plain sight, it’s virtually a home away from home for New England residents—the property even has a selection of tiny homes you can stay in—and a short train ride for those in the tri-state area.

For more than 150 years, Harper’s Bazaar has been the preeminent fashion and beauty resource for women at every age. We cover what’s new and what’s next in beauty by working with the world’s leading authorities in dermatology, plastic surgery, cosmetics, skincare, haircare, and fragrance. Every story we publish has been thoroughly researched and vetted by our team of beauty editors and industry experts.

#6880. Bob Dylan Dressed Like Timothée Chalamet to Meet the Pope

Today, millions of Americans awoke to social media feeds awash with images of and commentary about a landmark event that seemed nigh-impossible a mere 24 hours earlier. I speak, of course, of this image:

As the nation began to digest the results of a historic election that seemed to herald the arrival of a new America, two of our very best Americans met for a private audience on the other side of the Atlantic: Bob Dylan, the Great American artist, and Pope Leo XIV, the first American Pope. Dylan is in the midst of yet another lengthy European tour—his strongest in some number of years, judging by recent bootleg tapes. On November 2, he played a show at Milan’s Unipol Dome, then jetted over to Rome ahead of a November 5 performance at the ​​Palazzo dello Sport. What better way to kill an off-day than a quick chat with the Vicar of Christ?

It wasn’t the first time Bob met a Pope, as some pointed out. In 1997, he performed for John Paul II at the World Eucharistic Congress in Bologna, donning a dapper Western suit and a fine Stetson for the occasion. He and JP2 made for a bit of an odd couple, the mysterious American gunslinger and the seemingly-nonplussed Polish holy man.

Dylan has somewhat more in common with Pope Leo, a.k.a Robert Francis Prevost, a fellow Midwesterner. But even if the two men hail from similar geography, the sight of them together under the bright fluorescent lights of an anonymous Vatican office looked like the product of a well-crafted AI prompt, if not an outright fever dream. The images were so unexpected, so uncanny, I would’ve been convinced they were fake had they not been published by an official press organ of the Vatican.

Much of the whiplash stemmed from the two men’s appearances. Angel and devil. Spy versus spy. The man in the long black coat and the man in the short white mozzetta. The one liners wrote themselves.

On Wednesday, the Pope wore a clean white cassock and a tidy little zucchetto, with an iron cross hanging down around his neck. In short, he looked like the Pope. I suppose I would say the same about Bob: that he looked like himself. As any good Dylan fan knows, however, the meaning of that phrase has changed radically over the years. Bob’s shifting appearance is almost as notorious as his shifting artistic sensibility. Dust bowl refugee, sharp-suited mod, bohemian poltergeist, whatever this is—the man has worn many hats.

A hoodied Timothée Chalamet, seen with Kylie Jenner, in New York City.

Sometime in the early 1990s, Dylan took a liking to the humble hoodie, a stylistic evolution that intersected neatly with his booze-fueled artistic nadir. The hoodie allowed him to hide in plain sight, even as he continued to take the stage around the world on his Never Ending Tour. It was practical, yes, but it was also a defense mechanism, a way to synthesize his compulsion to perform and his aversion to being seen. That it often looked hard as hell did not hurt. (It should come as little surprise that Timothée Chalamet, who may as well have been born wearing a hoodie, seemed to riff on this era when he was channeling Bob all those months ago.)

In the 21st century, Bob came unstuck from time. On landmark records like “Love And Theft” and Modern Times, Dylan burrowed into an imaginary musical past situated somewhere between the Civil War and the Great Depression. His style shifted to match. In the early aughts, he adopted a full country-western guise, complete with a Lee Van Cleef mustache. In the years that followed, his sartorial inspirations shifted east: a little less cowboy, a little more riverboat gambler, often performing in wide-brimmed hats, long frock coats, and string ties. “Rock and roll Mark Twain” wouldn’t have been an inaccurate descriptor.

Which brings us back to today’s meeting with Pope Leo. On first glance, Dylan’s ensemble is bonkers: western black blouson with contrast stitching along the chest and collar; beneath that, a heavyweight black hoodie zipped to the chest; beneath that, a white collared shirt with several buttons undone, collar loosely drawn together with a polka-dot cravat. The hood is decidedly up, stationed atop a black flat brim cap. In some shots, Bob can even be seen sporting leather gloves and an old-fashioned umbrella, which seems to double as a cane.

Part “Man in Black” sobriety, part rock star debauchery, with a dash of the hypebeast flavor from Timmy’s A Complete Unknown press tour—the man got a damn fit off in Vatican City. There’s no other way to describe it. (It’s a shame Pope Leo left his Rhude jacket back at the Papal Palace.)

Still, it’s a look with roots in Bob’s past, or pasts: the hoodies of the ’90s, the Westernwear of the 2000s, even the polka dots of 1965. No one but Bob would ever dare to pair all these disparate elements, particularly for a private audience with the Bishop of Rome. No one but Bob could.

Someone should come up with a word for that.

#6875. Matt Damon on the Oscars, Tarantino, and the Roles That Got Away

In the desert, as the song goes, you can remember your name. So it’s fitting that Matt Damon has come here—to a sweltering stretch of inland California near Lancaster—to reflect on his 30-odd-year career as one of the most familiar and bankable faces in Hollywood, and specifically on a year that will go down as one of that career’s most challenging and rewarding passages.

Damon set 2026 off with The Rip, a throwback pulp thriller that reteamed him with his lifetime BFF Ben Affleck. He then went on to dominate the summer to a degree most actors crossing the other side of 55 wouldn’t dream of, with Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey—a noisy, thrilling, engrossing feat of big-budget moviemaking that wouldn’t have worked without an Odysseus compelling enough to command audiences to take the 20-year journey with him. The role demanded everything in Damon’s toolbox, and then some, but the result was a cultural and commercial run that only Spider-Man himself could top.

Jacket (sold as suit) by Norton & Sons. Shirt by Dolce & Gabbana. Pants by Celine. Cummerbund by Charvet. Pocket square by Budd Shirtmakers. Boots by Tom Ford.

This is rare air, and Damon knows it. Lightning struck once for him, with Good Will Hunting in 1997—he never thought he’d see that happen again, much less 29 years later. If exhaustion is the price of beating those odds, then so be it. Still readjusting to life on dry land, Damon sat down to unpack the most unlikely filmmaking experience of his career—and begin working out where he might want to go next.

This interview has been condensed and edited.

Matt Damon: That is amazing coming from him. Yes, I did hear about it.

Yeah, I think if you talk to any actor my age, any of the usual suspects, they’d probably say the same thing. I think the more you do it, if you’re working with really good people, you’re always improving, you’re getting better. And then you’re living more life and you have your family dynamics, all of your primary relationships kind of fill up your reservoir. I’ve talked to other actors my age about it, Ben included—I think there’s a tendency to push when you’re younger, a little bit, to try to work yourself up, and the older you get, the more available a lot of this stuff is. These very, very, very big emotions are kind of just sitting [near the surface]. And so you can really relax into the work, and it can be very full without being forced.

I think so, yeah. I mean, I’ve never been a part of a movie like The Odyssey that was…. Well, I guess Oppenheimer, to an extent, was somewhat of a phenomenon as well.

My workload was a lot heavier on this one, and everybody’s was, and that was kind of the joy of it. We knew we were the first ones to ever try to do a full film in IMAX [70 mm], and that comes with a whole host of challenges for every single department. We were all, I think, very patient with each other because we knew what was being asked of the other people. So in terms of the acting department—the IMAX load is two and a half minutes or three minutes. And so these big, heavy emotional scenes—you couldn’t play them all the way through. You’d have to cut them up. And the crew was just awesome, very patient with us. It felt like we were explorers, in a way. We got to be the first ones to do it, and that really bonded us.

I never would have expected it. And also, to play a role that’s so physical and, at my age, to even have a role that’s available on a scale like this. I’d always wanted to make a David Lean movie. To have it come at this point in my life, I felt incredibly lucky, not only to be able to do it, but also to be able to contextualize it. I don’t think I would’ve appreciated it as much 20 years ago.

No, I just don’t think I would’ve been able to understand and to hold as much gratitude as I am and as I did throughout the process. It’s all about how you look at things. And in retrospect, yeah, we were probably cold and wet. There was a lot of physical discomfort that was temporary. But because I felt so grateful to be there, I was just so excited. I was just so joyful the whole time that I didn’t really notice any of that.

[Laughs] There are no sure things, man. I think that’s where you get into trouble, being like, Well, this is a surefire hit. I’ve heard that before.

I felt it when I saw it. There is that thing that can happen where you make a movie, and you feel like it’s really good, and it just doesn’t connect, and there’s just nothing you can do about that. But as the audience reactions started to come in and the critical reactions were great as well, it was like, Okay….

Chris [Nolan], the most nervous he is in the entire process, from the very beginning to the very end, is the eve of the film coming out. He’s genuinely nervous. He talks openly about it, because that is the moment that you put it out into the world and you have to live with the reaction. Ultimately, the audience decides. Once it opened and the responses were what they were, I think I felt like, We’re going to be okay. It was a big bet in the sense that it was a big budget. I’m not used to playing in that arena. The numbers that movies have to get to just to break even at that level are just so beyond the movies that I’m known for.

A few of them. But The Martian, which I think is the biggest movie that I’ve ever headlined—I think The Odyssey made three times what that made. And that was a real hit for me. But there’s this other league of these kinds of movies that are in the billions that I’ve just never….

Well, the thing about a role like that is every single day I was paired with an unbelievable scene partner. One day I’d be working with Annie [Hathaway], and then the next day I’d be working with Himesh [Patel]. It’s following this character through all these different stages of his life and people who are close to him. So every day was a different, exciting little challenge, a little puzzle to figure out. I’ve always said that when you’re working with a great actor, they’re so good that they’re good enough for both of you. And it’s almost like this wave comes and you just stand up on your board and ride it. Then you have Samantha Morton, who literally just picks you up and shoulder-carries you.

It’s very hard to come in and do really intense things in a very short time frame. [Morton’s scenes]—that was under a week of shooting. Each day being incredibly intense. And that was what was so incredible about these actors who would come in. They’re under a lot of pressure. Nobody says that, but we all know the deal. These are critical scenes, and everybody just came in and killed it. It just got more and more exciting as we were going.

I agree. She’s incredible. She’s always been incredible. We had a near-miss working together in 2003—and I got to know her then—in a movie with Heath Ledger. I just think the world of her as an actress, and I was excited when we did the scene. I was like, This one’s going to really work. And I hope—it doesn’t always happen right away, but hopefully it’s lodged her back in the minds of people who are making those decisions, because she’s just extraordinary.

That’s the emotional spine of the movie. A lot of that monologue that I have at the end is in voiceover, but I wrote it all out and I had it on my phone and I would just carry it with me off set. I would just revisit it and run through it a few times, and I was doing that for months and months. Firstly, I thought it was just so beautifully written, but also because I knew that scene had to land. If that lands, then the movie works.

And that also means setting them up as a young couple before he leaves. There’s a lot of engineering that goes into getting to a scene like that and then you have to execute it. I spent an inordinate amount of time thinking about that one.

Well, at that point, the audience has been on the journey with you, and it’s been a hell of a journey, so you don’t have to tell them that. We were tired. That scene I shot with Samantha, oddly, we had come from Iceland, but we were shooting nights, and we came to Scotland, and I had it happen two or three times on this movie where I just didn’t sleep. I was tired and I went to bed, and I’m sure everyone’s had that feeling where you’ve got to get up the next day and you have something that you have to do and you can’t fall asleep and you’re kicking the blanket around and you’re staring at the ceiling. By the time I got to work to do the scene with Sam, I hadn’t slept in 24 hours, and by the end of the day it was 36 or whatever. I remember thinking the great thing about this character is he probably didn’t sleep last night. Don’t worry about it. So the weariness was very real for a lot of the movie.

Coat and pants by Dries Van Noten. Shirt by Dior. Cummerbund (throughout) by Charvet.

It’s all in there because it’s what I’m actually feeling. As I was saying before, it’s very easy to relax into what’s real and available and to have access to it.

This was the biggest one that we’ve ever done, and we’ve always had a two-week rule. I think the first stretch [on The Odyssey], I was away for three and a half weeks. The first half of the movie was the hardest because the kids were still in school. And then once the summer hit, they could come to set and we could be together. But that first stretch—luckily they’re huge Chris Nolan fans and they knew that it was this incredible role, so they were really supportive, and it was a sacrifice for them, and they were awesome about it.

He’s turned down a lot of work. And in fact, he really wanted to take on the CEO role at [Damon and Affleck’s production company] Artists Equity because it would keep him home. So when he directs—for instance, Animals, that was all shot in LA. He said to me when we started the company—he’s got joint custody, which means he gets to be with the kids half the time. And he said, “If I go to Estonia and do a movie, it’s not like I get that time back.” So that for him means he’s got to stay in LA.

I felt that way when I did Air with Ben. It was such a joyful experience, and we had so much fun, and we really loved the movie we made. We had this discussion about how I don’t want to do [a project] unless it’s joyful and unless it’s really right for my life. So much of being a young actor is you’re afraid it’s going to go away. You’re very much still in touch with that feeling of being unemployed. And so when Ben and I got famous, we both really worked. Buried ourselves in work. Partly because people are suddenly offering you things and you can’t imagine turning anything down, right? It just seems counter to everything you’ve been trying so hard to do for your entire life.

The first time was before The Bourne Identity came out. I had done The Legend of Bagger Vance with Will [Smith] and Charlize [Theron], and that movie didn’t do well. And Will was, as he is now, the biggest star. And so it’s like, it’s not Will’s fault. [Laughs] And then I did All the Pretty Horses with Billy Bob [Thornton], and that was a whole fraught experience. There were different ideas between the studio and between Billy Bob about what the movie should be, and so it ended up getting cut in a way that wasn’t to Billy’s liking and it didn’t do well. And so I had had these misses. And so there was this stretch where the phone just didn’t ring. The silence was…. It was a real rose-colored lenses coming off.

Well, the other thing was that The Bourne Identity was delayed something like a year. And so the word was, That’s going to be a stinker. But in that time, I went right from [filming] Bourne to Ocean’s Eleven. Then there was this whole period of time where nobody was calling, except for Steven Soderbergh, who called and offered me the movie The Informant! And I was so confused. I asked him, “Are you asking me to write it?” Because I couldn’t imagine he was asking me to be in it, because nobody else was calling. He goes, “What? No, Scott Burns is writing it. I want you to be the lead.” And I’ve never forgotten that. He just paid no attention to what everyone else was saying. But anyway, leaving that aside, nobody else was calling. And then The Bourne Identity came out, and it was a big hit.

It’s not personal. It’s business. And people can’t really go out of their way to help you. It’s really a pretty cold calculus that determines whether or not you’re on the list or off the list. And you never know who’s on the list. And the list can be different for different studios, who they’ll greenlight a movie with.

No, no. There’s very, very frank conversations between agents and studio people going like, “I can’t greenlight the movie with X, but I can with Y.” And that list is always changing, and I know that sometimes I’m on it and sometimes I’m not. I just have to remember it’s not personal, but you can feel it if you’re on the list or not.

Yeah. And [the] quality of projects [you’re offered]. I’m sure I’ve worked my way off that list a number of times. I’ve had some movies that didn’t work, and then you got to pay attention. I remember 2012 to 2014-ish, maybe, Patrick [Whitesell], my agent for 35 years, said I was taking a lot of supporting roles—because again, I’m not strategizing at all. I’m just like, Oh, True Grit. I want to work with the Coen brothers. Great.

Yup, George [Clooney directing], great. So there was this string of movies like that. Patrick called me and he said, “You’ll become a supporting actor. We got to think about this next one.” And then what happened to come along was The Martian. But that’s about as much strategizing as we’ve ever done.

Interstellar was in there too. I’m so happy that I’m in that movie. I love the role, even though it’s a tiny part. When Chris offered me [the role], we hadn’t worked together, and [when] he offered me the part in Interstellar, he said, “Do you know that saying ‘There are no small parts, there are only small actors’ ? This is a small part.” But I think he undersold it, because it’s a really kind of pivotal role, and I had a ball doing it.

Yeah, I think I was on a Bourne movie or about to go do Green Zone with Paul Greengrass. That and Milk. I was supposed to play the Dan White part. And I was really excited about it. I’d spoken to Sean [Penn], and I’d started the research. I’d been watching the documentaries about him. Then Sean’s schedule changed and the movie had to push three months, which meant that it was going to conflict with the movie I was going to do with Paul. So I had to bow out, and I just loved that script so much. And I love Gus [Van Sant], obviously. And so that was a bummer. But having said that, they got Josh Brolin, and Josh is absolutely brilliant in the movie. That helped a lot, I think, with this feeling that I have, that I do believe that the right actor gets the part.

I never like to say that, because I don’t want to live with the regret, but I can tell you that I passed on Ted, and I’ve never had this experience where I laughed so hard reading a script. I literally took it to dinner and was reading parts of it out to my friends at dinner. And then I just got too clever by half. I don’t know what I was thinking. I was like, Well, maybe the teddy bear’s actually the best part.…

So I overthought it and I talked myself out of it. And because it shot in Boston, it would’ve been a chance for me to be home—my father’s passed away now, and I look back and I go, Man, that would’ve been six months back [home].

But, again, the right actor got the part. Mark [Wahlberg’s] great, and the movie’s great, and I wouldn’t have made it any better.

They’re really hard to do. And I don’t read a lot [of them]. I don’t think people think of me for comedies a lot. But I love them, and I love watching them, and I love doing them if I can get my hands on one. But they’re few and far between. I think because they don’t travel well. They’re harder to get made because you really have to get all your money back in America. Comedy is so cultural. So the margin for error, I think, is smaller, which is what’s so great when you see a great one.

Anthony [Minghella], I think, was the first, with Talented Mr. Ripley. And he talked openly about it. A lot of these directors do. They want to use whatever it is that you have to offer in a new way and in a fun way and in an unexpected way. Some of those roles are the best roles that I’ve ever had a chance to play. Really interesting, complex people, and I love that.

I shot a scene in a movie theater with [Jack Nicholson], and Jack was working with an earwig. It was just the three of us before the crew came in, and we were just rehearsing. Marty [Scorsese] goes, “Let’s run this and see how we think it’s going to go.” And Jack took the earpiece out and we rehearsed the scene a few times and Jack was letter-perfect with his dialogue. And so Marty went to get the crew, and as people were coming in I said, “Hey, man, what’s the deal with the earwig? You obviously know all your lines—you’re writing stuff.” And it was really interesting because, he goes, “The older you get, the harder it is to remember your lines.” And that feeling of going up on a line, that panic, that surge of adrenaline that you get—he goes, “It just makes me so uncomfortable.” It’s a fail-safe. And if he knows [the earpiece is] there, his body stays in that relaxed state. People might think it was laziness. It was the opposite. He was a grinder. He worked really, really hard. It was more about just protecting himself.

No, no, no, no, no. I think everybody was really focused on Marty. And I remember I was shooting second unit for The Bourne Ultimatum during that Oscars, so I didn’t go. I was living in Miami at the time, and I had an infant. [My wife] Lucy and I were laying in bed watching it and so nervous for Marty. And then when he won, I was jumping up and down in the bedroom and I remember being really excited. And then the next category was best picture, and The Departed won. And I realized in that moment I hadn’t even considered that that could happen. And I turned to Lucy and I was like, “Shit, we should be there!” And then the next few weeks, I got all these calls from all the cast and the studio, like, “Hey, where were you, man?” I totally blew it. And then the next time that that happened was on Oppenheimer, and we had this whole scary thing happen where my wife had a brain aneurysm.

She’s totally fine, thank God, but [she] had this brain surgery the week before the Oscars. But it was the first night she felt good enough to get up and had her energy back. And she surprised me and got a dress and we went out to the after-party, because she remembered that the last time it happened, I didn’t do it. And she was like, “This time we’re going out.” And it was pretty fucking heroic of her to do that and really meaningful.

It’s not something you can control. You got to just do the work, and that’s got to be the reward. Because it’s like, then if you don’t win, do you feel bad about something that was really positive? Don’t turn this into something bad. It’s a tricky one. I always think about old movies when I look at them and it says, “So-and-so won an Oscar, so-and-so didn’t.” You’re judging it in a totally different way. It’s not attached to that anymore. I think the real way they should do them, really, is 20 or 25 years later. You’ll have a really good idea of which movies really endured and really still matter to you. There can be hype in the moment, or it can be a zeitgeist thing, but it doesn’t hold up. The goal for me is to make as many of those movies that are around 20 years later. That’s a cool thing. People come up [to me] about Good Will Hunting and I realize they weren’t born when the movie came out. It’s just very cool for Ben and me to have a new generation of people actually see it.

I don’t know what that means. I live in Brooklyn. [Laughs] If the trail is in that area, yeah. I don’t know what that entails. I will say, the technical side of this movie—I think of someone like Willie Burton, our sound guy, who’s in his 80s and he is a legend in the business.

And I did not loop a line in this movie, which is incredible. And I did not loop a line in Oppenheimer, which is incredible. Willie and Doug [Shamburger], his boom operator, are so good. Every scene on the ship, Willie is down below in the hold mixing in storms. The fact that he salvages all of that dialogue is nothing short of amazing. The technical achievement of our heads of department and their teams were really special. And I just hope that people realize that, realize what it took to do what they did, because from a technical standpoint, I hope they all get their flowers.

I didn’t think I was going to work. And then the Daniels called me and they had this project that was so out there, and it was such a Daniels movie and it’s so different from anything I’ve ever been able to do that I couldn’t pass it up. They’re really good people, and this movie’s like a cosmic love bomb for the world. So it felt like the right place to put my energy, but we’re almost done shooting it, and yeah, I’m going to take a long time off.

I haven’t thought about it. There are a lot of great performances. Paul Newman had some great ones at the end. We have the studio now with Artists Equity, and I feel like we’re just going to do stuff we really want to do, because it’s a very time-consuming job, and if you’re not 100 percent in, it’s just not worth doing. You have to really let it take over your life. I think that was one of the joys of The Odyssey—it dominated our lives.

There was a funny thing that happened on The Odyssey. We were in Scotland. I was with my kids. They’d just come over and gotten out of school, and we were eating dinner in this tiny hotel in this tiny town. And so there was one little dining room, which had 10 little tables in it, and the crew had watched dailies and so they were shuffling in, and I was already eating with my kids—I had jeans and a flannel shirt and a baseball hat on. And Chris came in and he walked by the table and I said, “Hey, Chris.” And he turned and he goes, “Oh, hey, Matt. I didn’t recognize you with the beard.” Chris doesn’t say stupid things—it was definitely the stupidest thing he’s ever said, because we’d been next to each other for six months.

He had just walked out of dailies watching close-ups of me, I’m sure with this giant beard. And I looked at him and he immediately realized what he’d said and he just looked at his shoes and then he looked up, like, Can we just agree that this never happened? And he shuffled off, and my kids go, “Dad, does Chris Nolan not know who you are?” I was like, “No, guys, he’s just exhausted.” He was that tired. He hadn’t seen me out of wardrobe in a while, you know what I mean? We were so tired by the end of that movie.

Well, he should write us a movie. We would be very happy if he did that.

Who knows? I have no right to expect it, let’s put it that way. I don’t want to sound like a broken record, but I have such gratitude for the fact that this came along my path at this point. I have no right to expect anything like that to happen again. But in the meantime, I just want to make good stuff and definitely keep working with Ben, because we’re at that point in our life where we’re like, “Fuck it, why not?” This was our dream as kids, as boys. We got to honor those boys and keep working together if we can. ✦

#6873. The 45 Best Movies to Watch With Your Parents Without Making It Weird

The family room has been declared an Awkward-Free Zone.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Home for the holidays? Living with your parents again? Welcome to the club... and not the fun kind of club that couldn't even handle you right now, once upon a time.

Let's say you and your roommates (aka mom and dad, maybe grandma and grandpa too) are looking for a quality movie to watch together that won't be filled with awkward scenes or jokes that only you laugh at while the dog side-eyes you from the carpet. It really puts a damper on your weekly Netflix scroll if you aren't careful. Nothing too artsy. Nothing you’ll have to explain. You want something adult, but not that kind of adult.

There's always a risk that family movie night implodes and ends with someone storming out of the room. Committing to the wrong movie is like opting to wear that cute pair of heels that always make your feet hurt even before you've left the Uber―painful and totally avoidable.

From action to comedy to classics, these movies will make your next family movie night more entertaining, less stressful, and an all-around good time. Just because a movie is the dreaded "family-friendly" doesn't mean it has to be boring or, like, for literal children. Swap your stilettos for your slippers, snuggle up on the couch, and start streaming.

You don't need to be a race-car fan (or know much about it in general) to enjoy this 2019 Best Picture nomination. An exhilarating film from both a storyline and videography standpoint, it stars your faves Christian Bale and Matt Damon. Lots of fast cars, personal demons, and some bromance.

This semi-autobiographical rock musical was written by Jonathan Larson before he found success with Rent and, subsequently, died far too young from an aortic dissection. But despite the real life tragedy, the movie is far from a bummer. Directed by Lin-Manuel Miranda and starring Andrew Garfield, it is an absolute joy and can offer your Sound of Music-loving family members a new musical to obsess over.

Don't listen to your older siblings: you're never, I mean never, too old for an animated Disney movie. Moana is something special; it’s a delightful story about embracing your identity and realizing your potential. And the music.

Isn’t it kind of crazy that this is rated PG-13? When a sequel not only lives up to, but also―dare I say―surpasses the first film, it's noteworthy. Meet the Fockers is one of the greatest sequels of all time. You also kind of NEED to watch before the next one, starring Ariana Grande, hits theaters.

Sure, you watched Ocean's Eleven back when it first came out in 2001, or caught the last 30 minutes on a hotel television once or twice. But when's the last time you re-watched it from start to finish? The legendary film about a casino heist is quick, witty, and action-packed. If the glossy crimes don't give you a rush of exhilaration and thrill, then Brad Pitt and George Clooney's dreamy faces sure will.

There's a lot to love about this movie and Ryan Gosling's abs are just six one of 'em. A quick warning, though. If you'd rather avoid a-l-l sex scenes when watching with your parentos, there are a few moments of Emma Stone and Ryan Gosling smooching in bed. And some of the power dynamics are questionable at best.

Next time you're stuck in Indecision Land, propose a fam trip to Middle-earth. Relatives of all ages will get lost in the magical world of Frodo, Gandalf, Gollum, and the whole gang. Even better, suggest a whole Lord of the Rings trilogy marathon―you’ll be set for the entire weekend.

The Family Stone often gets overlooked but it's a very touching story about acceptance and love. Especially good to watch around the holidays, it'll remind your blood relatives that every family has their own chaotic energy, not just yours. Also, not sure if you’ve heard, but there’s a sequel on the way!

Some days just call for a stress-free, aesthetically-pleasing, feel-good movie. Even better if it's about―you guessed it―food. Jon Favreau whips up a delicious film about the restaurant industry and family, but do not put this on the TV on an empty stomach. Actually, it'd be a fun one to make into a TV dinner situation.

You say "I want a heart-racing, invigorating movie." I say "Miracle." Hockey’s gotten randomly popular lately, so why not? It's about the 1980 U.S. men's Olympic hockey team and how they became a microcosm for American patriotism during the Cold War. It also has the greatest pump-up speech in cinematic history. Sometimes I listen to it when I'm feeling down and it makes me feel alive. Don't @ me, just watch.

Suspenseful, captivating, thrilling, and a true story? Argo tells the tale of one of the CIA's greatest top-secret missions that took place during the 1972 Iran Hostage Crisis. Ben Affleck stars in and directed this conversation starter of a film, which was nominated for *seven* Academy Awards and won three—including Best Picture.

An oldie but a goodie with Robin Williams and Nathan Lane. Hilarious at every moment. Once you meet Hank Azaria’s character, Agador Spartacus, you will never be the same. I laugh just thinking about it.

Is Albert Brennaman one of the greatest characters of all time? Probably not but his dance moves will go down in history. IYKYK, and if you don't, watch it.

Courtroom dramas and comedies are guaranteed crowd pleasers. When you've just about had it with your extended fam, turn on this movie and you'll be grateful Vinny isn't a relative. Unless...

If there's one thing guaranteed to please a crowd, it's a Tom-Hanks-and-Meg-Ryan romance. The classic tale of mistaken identity (and, oddly enough, the ins and outs of corporate book-selling) is charming enough that its take on the internet—chat rooms, lol—doesn't even seem annoyingly outdated, it just seems adorable. Set during the holiday season, it earns bonus points for cheer.

It's probably been way too long since anyone in your family sat down to watch the feature that put Matt Damon and Ben Affleck on the map, so why not rectify that together? Good Will Hunting even includes one of dearly departed Robin Williams's finest performances, in addition to wit and heart in equal measure.

The Oscar-nominated documentary might sound too cerebral for holiday consumption—it's about the tumultuous relationship between an artist and his wife—but the final product is touching, funny, and relatable.

An excellent movie series that the whole fam will get into. I mean, you can't go too long without re-watching the legend that is Katniss Everdeen. With two new installments, The Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes and Sunrise on the Reaping, this franchise is expanding in ways that demand more and more marathons.

This award-winning Sundance documentary will effectively cure any desire for your family to overspend on gifts this holiday (it's about one of America's richest families and their major money troubles), while also providing a screamingly funny inside look at a wacky, decadent lifestyle typically seen only on reality television.

Everyone loves Jennifer Lawrence. And Bradley Cooper. And Robert De Niro. And dancing. And sports. And an exciting competition.

James Bond is always a crowd-pleaser, and Daniel Craig's third entry into the ever-expanding 007 canon offers up both action and drama to spare. Plus: Adele.

This 2012 Oscar winner for Best Documentary is a full cinematic experience, and one that just so happens to sound damn good as it goes down. The feature chronicles some of music's most under-appreciated talents (back-up singers), and weaves in inspiring stories with some incredible music. Your entire family will be dancing in no time.

The amazingly talented Lake Bell wrote, directed, and starred in this hilarious 2013 comedy about the weird world of movie voiceover artists. Despite its highly specific subject matter, the film still works as an uproarious story about a gal just trying to make it. Even better? Her dad just happens to be the world's best voiceover guy, so it's got extra family resonance.

Do your parents not quite understand your current lifestyle choices? Introduce them to the incredibly charming (and slightly dunderheaded) Frances Ha. Odds are, they'll feel much better about your direction in life, even as they fall totally in love with the perfect creature that is Greta Gerwig.

There's no better way to follow up You've Got Mail than with yet another Hanks/Ryan pairing. Get out the hankies.

Sure, the Hayley Mills original is a classic, but this modern take on the old "separated at birth" story is funnier and flashier. It also features Lindsay Lohan in her most adorable role ever. Just prepare your most diplomatic answer for when your grandmother inevitably asks you, "What's that cute girl up to these days?"

It's an inspirational sports tale that feels appropriate for the season without actually compelling anyone to take up a new sport during this winter.

If you haven't seen the re-make Annie movie, now is the perfect time to catch up on hard-knock lives, fluffy puppies, and enough good cheer to make the entire family stand up and sing.

Use this time together to catch up on classic features that might have otherwise eluded you, including the kind of stuff that will send your grandparents swooning into their own memories of Audrey Hepburn, Gregory Peck, and the Italy of yesteryear.

It's probably been a minute since you saw the ultimate family bonding feature, and, yes, it's as good as you remember.

This fictionalized making-of-Mary Poppins movie is charming, heartbreaking, and will fill you and your parents' hearts with nostalgia while also teaching you something new about the woman who invented the practically perfect nanny.

Speaking of getting nostalgia shot straight to the heart, Marielle Heller's film about Fred Rogers and a cynical reporter who profiled him is kind of a masterpiece. They’re played by Tom Hanks and Matthew Rhys, respectively. The movie also chips away at toxic masculinity in a subtle way, if you want to come to family movie night with a low-key feminist agenda.

Greta Gerwig's adaptation highlights two of literature's best parents — insert Bob Odenkirk's "my little women" gif here — and revitalizes the classic in a way that will make it fresh for you and your parents.

Nothing says "dad movie" more than watching someone who's good at their job solve a problem. Ridley Scott's The Martian is two hours and twenty-four minutes of just that. Need I say more? It’s also technically a comedy.

Share a little history lesson with your folks. Pride is the story of a group of gay rights activists in 1980s London who go to a mining community to support a worker's strike because they have a common enemy in the cops that target them for harassment. The happy, hopeful ending has "feel-good" written all over it.

Sick of your parents talking about Hamilton to the point where you're pretty sure they don't actually get it? Why not steer them towards another musical that was filmed for a streaming network? Just make sure to have plenty of tissues on hand. You'll need them.

This is a nice and tremendously well-acted indie movie about the employees at a group home and the kids they look after. It also has a cast stacked with some of today's biggest stars. So if you actually like being IMDb for your parents and answering "where do I know them from?" every five minutes to show off your Hollywood trivia skills, this is the perfect movie for your fam.

Alternatively, take a peek into what your parents were like at your age and watch this Boomer (compliment, in this case) classic. Indulge them. It does have a great soundtrack. And since it’s streaming on Tubi, you can introduce them to their next favorite app. Parents love anything that’s free.

It's science fiction, but also a comedy, and accessible for the whole family. You don’t need to know anything about Star Trek or any other science fiction franchise. Galaxy Quest makes fun of Star Trek fans, but in a loving way.

Dads love westerns, and this is a neo-western. There is "brief" "sexuality" in Hell or High Water, so fair warning there... but I think you and your folks can handle it.

This coming-of-age comedy is about a girl who loves Dolly Parton and enters a local beauty pageant in order to get closer to her mother. It's super cute, romantic, a gret way to honor Dolly’s legacy, and will get your toe tapping to boot!

If you must watch a war movie with your parents, you might as well make it a war movie full of cameos from all your crushes to hold your attention. George MacKay! Andrew Scott! Richard Madden! Colin Firth! (It's also a very good movie with engaging action and an emotional plot, you know, if you care.)

Amma Asante’s period drama about a biracial women set to inherit a fortune in 18th century England, is based on a true story. It’ll satisfy your Bridgerton craving without making things awkward. Belle also, hint-hint and nudge-nudge, features a young James Norton and Sam Reid.

Do people still watch this coming-of-age story about four boys in rural West Virginia who learn how to build rockets in the 1960s? They should! Jake Gyllenhaal plays the leader. Laura Dern plays the teacher who believes in him. It’s such a charmer.

This movie offers romance for the women in your family and the opportunity to start listing facts about Superman actor Christopher Reeve for the men. Or maybe your family defies such traditional gender roles, and it’s the opposite or some combination of the two. What you’ll all get is a great movie experience.

#6870. All the Stars at the Harper’s Bazaar UK Women of the Year Awards

Including Mikey Madison, Cate Blanchett, and Isla Fisher

Here in the United States, Harper’s Bazaar hosts its annual Icons event, where this year’s honorees included none other than Céline Dion, Anna Sawai, Gabbriette, Charlotte Rampling, Cara Delevingne, Chris Fleming, Alysa Liu, Julie Mehretu, and Kim Gordon. But across the pond, our sister publication, Harper’s Bazaar UK, hosts its own awards ceremony, called Women of the Year, which celebrated its 2026 iteration last night at Claridge’s in London.

Like our “Icons” designation, the Women of the Year ceremony highlights figures who have inspired the world through art, entertainment, fashion, sports, and more. And the big winners of the evening included names like Oscar-winning actor Mikey Madison and All’s Fair star Glenn Close, the latter of whom was chosen as the Icon recipient for the WOTY ceremony.

Givenchy’s Sarah Burton was celebrated for her design, while Stephanie Cavalli was recognized for her modeling. Cate Blanchett, Sam Taylor-Johnson, Claudia Winkleman, Dina Asher-Smith, and Bella Maclean all walked home with statuettes as well.

Plus, a few other starry figures—like Isla Fisher, Elizabeth Debicki, and Letitia Wright—made surprise appearances.

Ahead, we round up all the celebrities who attended the 2026 Women of the Year Awards hosted by Harper’s Bazaar UK in London. Check out all the red-carpet photos below.

#6869. All About Luciana Damon, Matt Damon’s Wife of More Than 20 Years

Matt Damon may be one of Hollywood’s biggest stars, but his love story started far from a movie set.

The actor met his wife, Luciana Damon (née Barroso), in 2003 while filming Stuck on You in Miami. At the time, she was a bartender, and what began as an unexpected meeting eventually grew into a long-lasting marriage.

More than two decades later, the couple shares four daughters, has worked together professionally behind the scenes, and continues to make family a priority—even as Matt’s career takes him around the world. Most recently, Luciana and their daughters joined him at the London and New York premieres of Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey.

Here’s everything to know about Luciana Damon.

Luciana was born in Argentina and later moved to the United States.

While she has largely stayed out of the public eye throughout her marriage, she gave a rare interview to Vogue Australia in 2018, offering a glimpse into her family life and relationship with Matt.

Matt and Luciana first crossed paths in 2003 while he was filming Stuck on You in Miami.

Matt has often described it as love at first sight. During a 2011 appearance on The Ellen DeGeneres Show, he recalled, “I literally saw her across a crowded room, literally. Eight years and four kids later, that’s my life. I don’t know how else our paths would’ve crossed if that didn’t happen.”

Luciana remembers the evening a little differently.

“Matt’s story is that he saw me across the room, and there was a light on me. And I’m like, ‘Yeah, it was a nightclub—there were lights everywhere!’” she told Vogue Australia.

She explained that Matt came behind the bar after fans began approaching him for photos and autographs.

“He had started getting recognized and asked for pictures and autographs, and then it got kind of aggressive because people are drinking and stuff,” she said. “So he came and hid behind the bar...[wanting to] just hang out back there and have a drink. He says, ‘Oh, I saw you, and I really wanted to talk to you.’”

Rather than letting him stand around, she gave him a job.

“I said: ‘You know, if you’re going to be back here, you can’t just be standing there!’” she recalled. “He had trained as a bartender for a film years before, so he started making drinks. And he made me a ton of money in tips that night, because of course everyone wanted to go and see him. So it turned into a really fun night and then 15 years later, here we are!”

Luciana was raising her daughter, Alexia, from a previous relationship when she met Matt.

She told Vogue Australia that when Matt invited her to continue the evening with him and his friends, she declined because she needed to get home.

“I was like, ‘I can’t, I have a four-year-old daughter, I’m not going anywhere’...and that was one of the things he loved, that I had a daughter,” she said.

“He said, ‘I love that you’re a mom and that’s your priority.’ Some guys might have been different, they might think it’s complicated, but for him it wasn’t. When you meet somebody that you have a connection with, that’s just the person that you have a connection with, all the other stuff—the movie-star part—wasn’t really a factor. It was just Matt, to me he’s just Matt.”

The couple later welcomed three daughters together—Isabella, Gia, and Stella—and Matt has long considered Alexia his own daughter as well.

After dating for roughly two years, Matt and Luciana married in a private ceremony in December 2005.

Their wedding bands carry a particularly meaningful family tradition. Speaking to Parade in 2017, Matt explained that the rings contain tiny diamonds from his grandparents’ wedding ring.

“They weren’t quality diamonds; it was what [my grandfather] could afford,” he said. “But the marriage lasted. They were married for over 60 years. It wasn’t about the ring; it was about the marriage.”

“So when I told my mother that I was marrying Lucy, she gave me that ring and there were these six little diamonds. We took three of them and we put them on the inside of my band, and then we took three and we put them on the inside of her band. So they’re not visible, but we’re hopefully carrying the best part of that union with us.”

Over the years, Matt has shared one relationship rule that has helped their marriage endure.

“We have a two-week rule,” he told YourTango in 2021. “I’m not away for more than two weeks. I think you need to be with the person you love as much as possible. My wife is my soulmate. I don’t like being apart from her.”

There have been rare exceptions. While promoting Oppenheimer in 2023, Matt joked to Entertainment Weekly that he negotiated a break from acting with one exception.

“I had—not to get too personal—negotiated extensively with my wife that I was taking time off,” he said. “The one caveat to my taking time off was if Chris Nolan called.”

Matt has repeatedly credited Luciana with helping him navigate both his career and life.

During an interview with Jake’s Takes in 2023, he recalled feeling discouraged while filming a movie that wasn’t turning out the way he’d hoped.

“I remember my wife pulling me up because I fell into a depression,” he said.

Matt added, “I do pride myself, in a large part because of her, at being a professional actor. And what being a professional actor means is you go and you do the 15-hour day and give it absolutely everything, even in what you know is going to be a losing effort. If you can do that with the best possible attitude, then you’re a pro. And she really helped me with that.”

Although Luciana wasn’t part of the entertainment industry when she met Matt, she’s taken on a larger role behind the camera in recent years.

She served as an executive producer on The Instigators in 2024 and produced The Rip in 2026.

Speaking with Entertainment Tonight at The Instigators premiere, Matt said the producing credit had been years in the making.

“She’s been reading every script and watching every cut of every movie for 21 years. This is the first time we ever kind of codified it,” he said.

He added that Luciana initially resisted taking credit for her work.

“She didn’t even want to take the credit. She was there every day, she was doing all this work on the script, and then she was like, ‘Nah, I don’t want to take the credit.’”

“The other producers on the movie...came of their own volition [and said], ‘If you’re not taking credit, we’re not taking credit.’ It was very sweet. She succumbed and took the credit.”

Luciana has rarely spoken publicly about her marriage, but when Vogue Australia asked about the secret to a lasting relationship, her answer was refreshingly simple.

“I just know I think we both feel, really, really lucky to have met each other so we don’t take that for granted. It’s life and marriage, so there’s ups and downs you know, but overall it’s easy and it’s fun. And he’s such a good dad.”

In a November 2026 interview with GQ, Matt revealed that Luciana underwent brain surgery after experiencing a brain aneurysm in 2024.

“She’s totally fine, thank God, but [she] had this brain surgery the week before the Oscars,” he said.

Although the couple missed the ceremony, Luciana surprised Matt by getting dressed up to attend an Oscars after-party once she was feeling better.

“But it was the first night she felt good enough to get up and had her energy back. And she surprised me and got a dress and we went out to the after-party, because she remembered that the last time it happened, I didn’t do it. And she was like, ‘This time we’re going out.’ And it was pretty fucking heroic of her to do that and really meaningful,” Damon recalled.

#6868. Darian Mensah Might Be the Richest Player in College Football. But He’s Still Going to Class

A decade ago, an interview with the quarterback of the unbeaten, top-ranked team in college football might have involved the player, the reporter, and the team’s sports information director. Maybe the player’s dad stood nearby, keeping guard. But when I talked to Darian Mensah on the Sunday before Halloween, there were five other people on the call, plus a few more sitting in the room with the Miami Hurricanes’ smooth but press-shy signal-caller.

Mensah is the most modern sort of college star, a two-time transfer now at his third school who has navigated a bevy of NIL deals. “I’ve got a great group of people who I think truly want to see me win,” he tells me.

The most notable among them is Malachi Toney, the sophomore receiver who caught 16 balls from Mensah the day before we spoke. By throwing tons of on-target passes, Mensah is comfortably pacing to set college football’s single-season record for completion percentage. As the Canes get ready to travel to South Bend to face No. 4 Notre Dame this weekend, the 21-year-old Heisman Trophy frontrunner is at 80.7 percent, several points clear of second place all-time.

At first glance, Mensah has a simple job: just throw the ball to Toney, who has unparalleled open-field maneuverability. In a 52-point outing against Pittsburgh last month, the two accounted for 194 yards and three way-too-easy scores. “I mean, on the first touchdown, I gave a little signal to Mali,” Mensah recalls. “You can see there's only one guy over the pod of three guys.” The film confirms it: Pitt didn’t put enough defenders near Toney, and Mensah identified it. “You got to keep going to it,” he says.

Mensah loves talking football, though he’s just as happy to get in-depth about how chaotic his life is right now. Mensah is trying to deliver Miami back to its glory days as he manages a variety of commercial arrangements, prepares for his professional future, and, yes, even goes to class, contrary to popular ideas about the NCAA football player experience. “I have to go to class,” he confesses. “Transferring [two] times kind of messed with my credits.” He has three classes per day, on Tuesdays and Thursdays, and has had to keep himself eligible at Tulane (which “set the standard for me”), Duke (“probably even a little bit harder”) and now Miami: “Balancing that, school and football, was tough. But I think it set me up for where I'm at now, where maybe it's not as rigorous as far as academics go.”

Mensah gets particularly excited to discuss fashion, which I bring up because most athletes who sit for GQ have at least a moderate enthusiasm for clothes. Mensah, for what it’s worth, is wearing a cropped Adidas T-shirt—the brand is one of his many partners, alongside Dr Pepper and Sling TV. Miami has a roughly $50 million roster, one of the five biggest budgets in the sport. And Mensah, as the team’s star quarterback, is probably its top earner. But I wondered, more specifically, how much money Mensah is set to earn this year between football and his various NIL agreements. “I don’t know,” he demures. “As much as God allows me to.”

Market rate for a Power 4 starting QB is in the low to mid-seven figures, and Mensah is almost certainly on the high end of that spectrum. I’m not interested in pocket-watching, but just a few weeks ago, the United States did pass a bill that would cap how much someone like Mensah can make. The “Protect College Sports Act,” a bill developed by Sen. Ted Cruz and his Democratic counterpart, Maria Cantwell, may come up for a House vote in the upcoming lame-duck session of Congress. (It passed the Senate with a bipartisan array of 77 votes.) The bill would cap school pay to athletes at about $50 million per university, essentially freezing the market right where Miami’s football team currently stands.

The bill’s backers, including seven-time National Champion Nick Saban, have argued that college football player pay is out of control and endangers the integrity of college sports writ large. Essentially, the bill is coming for Mensah’s money, more than practically anyone else among the hundreds of thousands of NCAA athletes. So how does that sit with him?

“I grew up in a single-parent household, and my mom providing for all my siblings was not easy,” he explains. “So to be able to take care of my family in a way that I wasn't able to before has only been a blessing. I don't really know what to say about that other than I'm blessed to be in the position I'm in.”

Mensah doesn’t preoccupy himself with the weight of Miami football's illustrious tradition. He knows the program was “like a dynasty and a powerhouse back then,” and he’s gotten to meet plenty of legends of The U. “Me and the guys always strive to follow in their footsteps,” he says. “But yeah, I was born in 2005...”

In his relatively short life, Mensah has packed in a lot. After playing high school ball in Santa Maria, California, he started his college career as a three-star true freshman at Tulane in 2024. His only other college offer, he told me, was from FCS Idaho State. He entered the transfer portal to play at Duke in 2025, then reentered after winning the ACC Championship last year. In addition to climbing the economic ladder, Mensah has lived in four cities over the past four years. He’s only 21, but he’s got the palette of a washed-up 32-year-old like me. Maple Street Café in New Orleans, which has a chicken lemon dish that is “some of the most amazing pasta I’ve ever had in my life,” is a standout.

Come 2027, there’s a good chance Mensah will make it five cities in five years. He’s eligible for the NFL draft, and should he declare, he’s expected to be taken early in the first round by one of the league’s many QB-needy teams. But I’m a college football guy at heart, so near the end of our call I ask Mensah what he thinks of the impending 24-team playoff format that’s likely to be instituted next season. "If everything goes to plan," he says, "it won't really concern me." Why not, I ask?

#6867. The Black Doves Season 2 Finale Has an Insane Cliffhanger

Let’s talk about Helen Webb and what it all means for season 3.

After a two-year wait, Netflix's Christmas crime caper Black Doves is finally back for its second outing. It's a messy delight that tests the loyalties of the central characters in ways the first season never dared and—both annoyingly and excitingly—ends on just as agonizing a cliffhanger.

In the first season, Elizabeth Reed (Sarah Lancashire) was something of a mystery: an ominous, witty controller of sorts who kept Helen Webb (Keira Knightley) and Sam Young (Ben Whishaw) on their toes. But this time around, Black Doves spends some time unravelling her past, as well as that of the titular intelligence agency: a backstory that informs and explains the major threat of the season, the German anarchist organization Kestrel.

Though the secrets reveal themselves slowly, a bombshell-filled (pun very much intended) finale crashes through a series of twists that—if you didn't see them coming (which I can't say I did, for some of them)—will really feel like a stab in the face. No, not the back, because unfortunately the answers were always lying right in front of us.

There's a lot to break down from that last episode, so read on for our recap of Black Doves season 2 and an explanation of the ending—including that twist that'll probably have a drastic impact on where we'll be at the beginning of season 3.

The penultimate episode ended with a bunch of crossed wires, conflicting contracts, and potential deaths. Eleanor and Williams have taken out paid hits on Laila and Sam, while Sam's final target in his prison-break contract with Kestrel is none other than Reed (after episode 4 revealed that she is in fact the mysterious “Wildflower” of the files she tried to take from Alexi Chernov).

The main action kicks off when the precious international peace summit that Wallace (Andrew Buchan) has spent the whole season planning gets upended before it even starts. Wallace and Helen's cab is intercepted and chased, while U. S. defense envoy Cecile Mason (Neve Campbell) and recently widowed Russian envoy Katia Chernov (Sylvia Hoeks) are captured by strange armed men.

When Patrick (Sam Riley), the Kestrel man who's been blackmailing Sam all season, escorts Reed back into the Black Doves office with a gun to her head, it quickly becomes clear that 'Wildflower', or Reed, is the only target Kestrel wants brought in alive. Or, at least, whoever's running it does.

And there's the twist: after a horrid little hostage situation in the office results in the deaths of quite a few senior Black Doves and Mason, Katia reveals that she is in fact the head of Kestrel and, in turn, Vita—the long-lost, abandoned daughter that Reed left in Berlin after killing her fake husband and running off (ouch).

Dani, the nuisance of a secretary Reed planted in Wallace's office to seduce him and potentially replace Helen, has ended up being recruited by Kestrel. But she doesn't last long after a game of grenade hot potato played by Eleanor, Williams, and Laila ends with Dani being caught in a lift with it. Even Reed's capable of mistakes.

Meanwhile, over in the car park where Helen and Wallace end up after fleeing, Mr. Prime Minister finally decides to question his wife about her past and her secrets. Obviously, she lies again—until the situation shifts a little.

Helen receives a series of ominous messages and threatening instructions, first over text, then through a discreet earpiece planted in her car. Katia, whom Helen very quickly realizes is not an ally, instructs her to kill Wallace and turn herself in to MI5. But what's the leverage, you ask?

Marie, the housekeeper who was teased as a Black Dove at the end of the first season, has in fact been working for Kestrel this whole time. If you're a babysitter to the Prime Minister's spy wife, who's been planted by a capitalist intelligence agency, but you're working for a German anarchist organization, does that make you a double or a triple agent? A nightmare for Mrs Webb, more than anything.

Helen tricks Katia over the phone by shooting past Wallace towards the window, explains (on a surface level) that the children are in danger and she needs to hijack the car, and darts off with Sam to rescue the children from Marie.

After confronting Marie, Helen shoots her in the head and flees the scene, while Reed reconciles (not quite) with her bleeding-out daughter in her last moments. Kestrel's down and all is well? Obviously not, because it's never that simple.

MI5 is even more suspicious of Helen than ever before, but Wallace is covering for her. Conteh, as one of the few surviving senior members of the Black Doves, restores Reed to her former standing.

Helen and Sam reconcile properly after their heated argument during the first Reed rescue mission of the season, and he reveals that he's going to leave the country once again. After they part, a strange man bumps into Helen and, BOOM, final twist! Patrick has escaped in perfect condition and Kestrel is still going strong. He leaves Helen with his card, more as a threat than anything else.

Reed and Helen agree to increase Helen's standing and role within the Black Doves, meaning that she'll be more than just an intel-grabber going forward, and putting her in a pretty good spot work-wise. However, things aren't quite so simple at home.

Wallace confronts Helen once again about her real name (Lily Morris), and the finale ends with what appears to be the beginning of a conversation about her past. But how much is Helen actually going to tell Wallace? There's only so much she can give away without completely compromising her position, but she'll have to say enough to regain his trust. We'll find out in two years, I suppose.

#6866. Meet the Husbands, Partners, and Exes of the ‘Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County’ Cast

Exactly one week before the premiere of The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County, OG MomTok member Jen Affleck shocked fans by filing for divorce from her husband of seven years, Zac Affleck. Of course, if you’ve been paying attention, then this update isn’t all that surprising…save for the fact that Jen is currently pregnant with her and Zac’s fourth child. And while fans have been predicting this split since season 1 of SLOMW, chances are that there will be even more tension between Jen and Zac in this new spinoff series.

Wondering what other kind of relationship drama SLOMW:OC will bring? While most of the new cast members are in long-term, committed marriages, you can look forward to seeing the dynamic of Aspyn Ovard’s buzzy throuple with Billy and Bri Davis firsthand, as well as Bobbi Althoff’s budding romance with basketball player Tyler Hawkins. Here’s a rundown on everyone’s relationships ahead of the November 12 premiere.

Current relationship status: Married to Nathan Pease

Ashleigh tied the knot with Nate in September 2013, and four kids later, they still seem rock-solid.

Current relationship status: Dating Billy Davis and Bri Davis

Just about two years filing for divorce from Parker Ferris in April 2024, Aspyn made headlines when she revealed she was in a relationship with married couple Billy and Bri Davis, who practice consensual non-monogamy. She later explained that the relationship started with just Bri before she became involved with Billy as well.

Current relationship status: Married to David Woods

Avery married former police officer David back in June 2016 and they still seem very much in love. David also has two kids from a previous relationship.

Current relationship status: Dating Tyler Hawkins

Once her divorce from Cory Althoff was finalized in August 2024, Bobbi struck up a romance with basketball player Tyler Hawkins in November 2024, per People, and two years later, they’re still going strong.

Current relationship status: Married to Dylan Higginson

Despite getting her role reduced since the initial cast announcement, it seems like Chandler and her husband Dylan (who she’s been married to since February 2016) will still make some appearances.

Current relationship status: Divorcing Zac Affleck

Number of kids: 3 (with a fourth on the way)

After seven years of marriage (and MONTHS of breakup rumors), TMZ broke the news that Jen had filed for divorce from Zac. The two tied the knot in June 2019 after meeting on the Mormon dating app Mutual the year before.

Current relationship status: Married to Corey McClanahan

Jordan and her firefighter husband Corey (who were high school sweethearts!) have been married since February 2017 and are seemingly still head-over-heels for each other.

Current relationship status: Married to Kyler Steven Fisher

Madison got married to Kyler back in April 2013 after meeting on Facebook, and now they share five kids and a wildly popular YouTube channel.

Current relationship status: Married to Kirk DaPron

McCall and Kirk have been married since September 2011 and (though they recently faced backlash for their controversial family Halloween costume) are still loved up 15 years later.

Current relationship status: Married to Aaron Brimhall

Number of kids: 2 (with a third on the way)

Despite breakup rumors, Salomé and her photographer husband Aaron (who she married in December 2012) are still together and gearing up to welcome their third child.

#6865. Travis Kelce Is Excited to Celebrate His First Christmas “As a Married Man”

The couple is getting ready for their first holiday season as Mr. and Mrs. Swift

Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce are fully in their newlywed era. After getting married in New York’s Madison Square Garden over Fourth of July weekend, the two are getting ready to celebrate their first holiday season as a married couple—and the Kansas City Chiefs tight end is beyond looking forward to it.

“I’m excited for my first holiday as a married man,” Kelce told Women’s Wear Daily. “I love all the old traditions and can’t wait to start some new ones. I start celebrating as that tree goes up.”

In a promotional video for the Tommy Hilfiger winter 2026 campaign, shared on Instagram, Kelce spoke on the personal traditions that make up his “perfect holiday.”

The NFL star said, “My mother, [Donna Kelce], baking blueberry muffins.” He later joked, “You got any Tommy Christmas onesies somewhere?”

Last year, the couple attended a pre-Christmas family party in Kansas City, Missouri, with members of the Kelce family. They then spent the actual Christmas Day at Arrowhead Stadium, where Kelce and his Chiefs played the Denver Broncos. The pop superstar’s parents, Scott and Andrea Swift, joined her for the game to make it a full family affair. And after the game, Swift and Kelce traveled to the mountains for a cozy winter getaway. Who knows what they have planned for this year, now that they are officially Mr. and Mrs. Swift-Kelce.

While the “Opalite” singer has always maintained that her favorite holiday is Fourth of July, it seems the Swift-Kelces’s holiday of choice may now be Christmas.
